Conjunctive Queries with Negation and Aggregation: A Linear Time Characterization
Hangdong Zhao, Austen Z. Fan, Xiating Ouyang, Paraschos Koutris

Abstract
. In this paper, we study the complexity of evaluating Conjunctive Queries with negation ( 𝖢𝖰 ¬ \mathsf{CQ}^{\neg} ). First, we present an algorithm with linear preprocessing time and constant delay enumeration for a class of CQs with negation called free-connex signed-acyclic queries. We show that no other queries admit such an algorithm subject to lower bound conjectures. Second, we extend our algorithm to Conjunctive Queries with negation and aggregation over a general semiring, which we call Functional Aggregate Queries with negation ( 𝖥𝖠𝖰 ¬ \mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg} ). Such an algorithm achieves constant delay enumeration for the same class of queries, but with a slightly increased preprocessing time which includes an inverse Ackermann function. We show that this surprising appearance of the Ackermmann function is probably unavoidable for general semirings, but can be removed when the semiring has specific structure. Finally, we show an application of our results to computing the difference of CQs.



1.
Introduction

This paper focuses on the query evaluation problem for Conjunctive Queries with negation (
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
), a fundamental class of relational queries. We will think of a
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
as having the following form:

(1)

\displaystyle Q(\mathbf{x}_{F})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}}R_{K}(\mathbf{x}_{K})\wedge\bigwedge_{K\in\mathcal{E}^{-}}\neg R_{K}(\mathbf{x}_{K})

where the variables are of the form
x_{i}
,
i\in[n]=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
,
\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-}
are two sets of hyperedges that are subsets of
[n]
, and

F\subseteq[n]
are the free variables. We will call the triple
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
the
signed hypergraph
of
Q
.
We will consider only
safe queries
, where
\bigcup_{K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}}K=[n]
.
When
\mathcal{E}^{-}=\emptyset
, then
Q
is a Conjunctive Query (CQ) with the associated hypergraph
([n],\mathcal{E}^{+})
.

The complexity of query evaluation for CQs is well-understood. Yannakakis
(
Yannakakis 1981
)
first showed that a Boolean CQ (i.e.,
F=\emptyset
) can be evaluated
on a database
\mathcal{D}
of size
|\mathcal{D}|
in time
O(|Q|\cdot|\mathcal{D}|)
if the hypergraph
([n],\mathcal{E}^{+})
is
\alpha
-acyclic (
|Q|
denotes the size of the query). Further work
(
Bagan et al. 2007
;
Berkholz et al. 2020
)
generalized this result to show that if
Q
is
free-connex
\alpha
-acyclic
then the tuples in
Q(\mathcal{D})
can be enumerated with constant delay
O(|Q|)
after a linear-time preprocessing step. Free-connex
\alpha
-acyclicity means that both
([n],\mathcal{E}^{+})
and
([n],\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\{F\})
are
\alpha
-acyclic hypergraphs. It was also shown
(
Bagan et al. 2007
)
that this tractability result is tight under widely believed lower-bound conjectures.

1.1.
CQs with Negation

Our first goal in this paper is to generalize the above classic result to the case where
\mathcal{E}^{-}\neq\emptyset
. Prior work has looked into this problem, but without achieving a complete answer.

To explain the current progress, let us first consider the case of a Boolean
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
and attempt to solve the (seemingly harder) problem of counting the number of valuations that satisfy the body of
Q
with input a database
\mathcal{D}
, which we will denote as
\#Q(\mathcal{D})
. We should note here that
\#Q
is solvable in
O(|\mathcal{D}|)
time if
Q
is an
\alpha
-acyclic CQ. Brault-Baron
(
Brault-Baron 2013
)
had the insight that we can compute
\#Q
using the inclusion-exclusion principle. Indeed, let
Q_{S}
be the Boolean CQ with hypergraph
([n],\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup S)
for any
S\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}
. Then we can write:

(2)

\displaystyle\#Q(\mathcal{D})=\sum_{S\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}}(-1)^{|S|}\#Q_{S}(\mathcal{D})

Hence, if every
Q_{S}
is
\alpha
-acyclic, then
\#Q
(and thus
Q
) can be computed with data complexity
O(|\mathcal{D}|)
. This naturally leads to the notion of
signed acyclicity
, introduced in
(
Brault-Baron 2013
)
: a Boolean
CQ^{\neg}
is signed-acyclic if the hypergraph
([n],\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup S)
is
\alpha
-acyclic for every
S\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}
. Thus, a Boolean
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
can be computed in linear time (data complexity) if it is signed-acyclic. Interestingly, if
\mathcal{E}^{+}
consists only of singleton hyperedges, then signed acyclicity is equivalent to
\beta
-acyclicity of the hypergraph
([n],\mathcal{E}^{-})
. However, there are two issues with applying the inclusion-exclusion approach. First, it has an exponential dependency on
|Q|
and thus does not give a polynomial-time algorithm in combined complexity. Second, it cannot be used to provide any delay guarantees for the enumeration problem in non-Boolean queries.

The second issue was partially addressed by Brault-Baron
(
Brault-Baron 2012
;
Brault-Baron 2013
)
, who proposed an enumeration algorithm for any free-connex signed-acyclic
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
. However, this algorithm either achieves constant delay with a
O(|\mathcal{D}|\log^{|Q|}|\mathcal{D}|)
preprocessing time, or achieves logarithmic delay with linear preprocessing time. The logarithmic factor is a consequence of the technique used, which translates a database instance to an instance over the Boolean domain.

Our first main result shows that the translation to the Boolean domain is not necessary and in fact we can achieve both constant delay and linear time preprocessing for free-connex signed-acyclic queries. Moreover, our algorithm has only a polynomial dependence on the size of the query.

Theorem 1.1.

Let
Q
be a free-connex signed-acyclic
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
. Then there is an algorithm that can enumerate the results of
Q(\mathcal{D})
with
O(|Q|^{3}+{|Q|}\cdot|\mathcal{D}|)
preprocessing time and
O(|Q|)
delay.

1.2.
FAQ with Negation

Our second goal is to study the evaluation of
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
in the presence of aggregation. We do this by studying a more general problem, that of computing a
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
under a general semiring, following the approach of
FAQ
s
(
Khamis et al. 2016
)
. More precisely, given a commutative semiring
\mathbf{S}=(\boldsymbol{D},\oplus,\otimes,\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1})
, we define an
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
as an expression of the form:

(3)

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{F})=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{x}_{[n]\setminus F}}\;\bigotimes_{K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}}R_{K}(\mathbf{x}_{K})\otimes\bigotimes_{N\in\mathcal{E}^{-}}\overline{R}_{N}(\mathbf{x}_{N}),

Here, a
positive factor

R_{K}
can be viewed as a table of entries of the form
\langle\mathbf{x}_{K},R_{K}(\mathbf{x}_{K})\rangle
(where the weight of tuple
\mathbf{x}_{K}
is the value
R_{K}(\mathbf{x}_{K})\in\boldsymbol{D}
), and for entries not in the table, the weight is implicitly
\mathbf{0}
. On the other hand, a
negative factor

\overline{R}_{N}
is a table of entries of the form
\langle\mathbf{x}_{K},R_{N}(\mathbf{x}_{N})\rangle
, and for entries not in the table, the weight is a default constant value
\mathbf{c}_{N}\neq\mathbf{0}
. To recover the
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
setting, we choose the Boolean semiring, and encode the values of the negative factor such that it is
\mathbf{0}
if the tuple is in the table, otherwise
\mathbf{1}
. Our semiring formulation is though much more general: the only difference between a negative and a positive factor in our setting is whether the "default" value of a tuple not in the table is
\mathbf{0}
. This observation offers a novel angle to the semantics of negation, and is critical to our algorithms.
For
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
, we can show the following.

Theorem 1.2.

Let
\varphi
be a free-connex signed-acyclic
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
query over a commutative semiring
\mathbf{S}
. Then there is an algorithm that can enumerate
\varphi
with
O(|\varphi|^{3}+{|\varphi|}\cdot|\mathcal{D}|\cdot\alpha(14\cdot|\mathcal{D}|,|\mathcal{D}|))
preprocessing time and
O(|\varphi|)
delay.

Here,
|\varphi|
denotes the size of the query
\varphi
and
\alpha(m,n)
denotes the inverse Ackermann function, which grows extremely slowly as a bi-variate function of
m,n
. The appearance of this function in the runtime expression is surprising in our opinion. It occurs because the aggregation problem reduces to the well-studied problem of computing interval sums over an arbitrary semiring, called
\mathsf{RangeSum}

(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
;
Yao 1982
)
. If the semiring structure allows for linear preprocessing and constant-time answering for its
\mathsf{RangeSum}
problem, then we can drop the Ackermann factor. Examples of such semirings are the Boolean semiring
(\{\textsf{true},\textsf{false}\},\vee,\wedge,\textsf{false},\textsf{true})
, tropical semiring
(\mathbb{R},\min,+,+\infty,0)
, and semirings with additive inverse (e.g. the counting ring over integers, i.e.
(\mathbb{Z},+,\times,0,1)
which we use to count solutions).

Theorem 1.3.

Let
\varphi
be a free-connex signed-acyclic
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
query over a commutative semiring
\mathbf{S}
with additive inverse. Then there is an algorithm that can enumerate
\varphi
with
O(|\varphi|^{3}+{|\varphi|}\cdot|\mathcal{D}|)
preprocessing time and
O(|\varphi|)
delay.

1.3.
Lower Bounds

Our third goal is to match our linear-time upper bounds with lower bounds. In this direction, we show that under believable conjectures, any
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
that is not free-connex
signed
-acyclic does not admit an algorithm that can emit the first result in linear time (hence matching the upper bound). Our conditional lower bounds are somewhat weaker than the ones obtained for CQs because the presence of negation means that we cannot use the sparse version of some problems (e.g., detecting a triangle, or Boolean matrix multiplication). For
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
, we show stronger conditional lower bounds over the tropical semiring and counting ring based on weaker lower bound conjectures. Finally, we provide some evidence that the inverse Ackermann factor in the runtime for general semirings is unavoidable. In particular, we show that the query

\varphi(x)=\bigoplus_{y}A(x)\otimes B(y)\otimes\overline{R}(x,y)

corresponds to a variant of the offline
\mathsf{RangeSum}
problem over a general
\oplus
operator. Using this observation, we can modify a construction of Chazelle
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
to show a superlinear lower bound on the number of
\oplus
operations necessary to compute
\varphi
.

1.4.
Difference of CQs

Finally, we show that our algorithm for
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
can be applied to obtain optimal algorithms with linear-time preprocessing and constant delay for the problem of computing the difference between two CQs of the form
Q_{1}-Q_{2}
, which was recently studied by Hu and Wang
(
Hu and Wang 2023
)
.

2.
Preliminaries

Hypergraphs.

A hypergraph is a pair
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E})
where
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
is the set of vertices of
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{E}
is a multiset
1
1

1

A multiset is a collection of elements each of which can occur multiple times
of hyperedges where each
K\in\mathcal{E}
is a nonempty subset of
[n]
.

A signed hypergraph is a tuple
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
where
[n]
is the set of vertices of
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{E}^{+}
and
\mathcal{E}^{-}
are two multisets of hyperedges where each hyperedge
K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
(resp.
N\in\mathcal{E}^{-}
) is a subset of
[n]
. We consider only
safe
signed hypergraphs, where every vertex in
[n]
occurs in some hyperedge
K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
.

Semigroups and semirings.

A triple
\mathbf{S}=(\boldsymbol{D},\oplus)
is a semigroup if
\oplus
is an associative binary operator over
\boldsymbol{D}
.
A tuple
\mathbf{S}=(\boldsymbol{D},\oplus,\otimes,\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1})
is a (commutative) semiring
if
\oplus
and
\otimes
are commutative binary operators over
\boldsymbol{D}
satisfying the following conditions:
(1)
(\boldsymbol{D},\oplus)
is a commutative monoid
2
2

2

In the usual semiring definition, we do not need the multiplicative monoid to be commutative.
with an additive identity, denoted by
\mathbf{0}
; (2)
(\boldsymbol{D},\otimes)
is a commutative monoid with a multiplicative identity, denoted by
\mathbf{1}
; (3)
\otimes
distributes over
\oplus
; and (4) For any element
e\in\boldsymbol{D}
, we have
e\otimes\mathbf{0}=\mathbf{0}\otimes e=\mathbf{0}
.

Enumeration and Complexity.

In this paper, we study the enumeration problem for
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
queries,
\mathsf{Enum}(\varphi,\mathcal{D})
, which takes as input a
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
query
\varphi
and a database instance
\mathcal{D}
and outputs a sequence of answers such that every
answer
in
\varphi(\mathcal{D})
is printed precisely once.
An enumeration algorithm for
\mathsf{Enum}(\varphi,\mathcal{D})
may consist of two phases:

•

(preprocessing phase) it constructs efficient data structures from
\varphi
and
\mathcal{D}
; and

•

(enumeration phase) it may access the data structures built during preprocessing, and emit the answers of
\varphi(\mathcal{D})
one by one, without repetitions.

We say that an enumeration algorithm enumerates with delay
O(\tau)
if the time between the emission of any two consecutive answers (and the time to emit the first answer, and the time from the last answer to the end) is bounded by
O(\tau)
. In particular, we say that an enumeration algorithm is
constant-delay
if it enumerates with delay independent of the input database size
|\mathcal{D}|
.

Model of computation.

We adopt the
random-access machine (RAM)
as our computation model with
O(\log n)
-bit
words, which is standard in fine-grained complexity. The machine has read-only input registers and it contains the database and the query, read-write work memory registers, and write-only output registers. It is assumed that each register can store any tuple, and each tuple is stored in one register. The machine can perform all ‘‘standard’’
3
3

3

This includes all arithmetic (e.g.
+,-,\div,*
) and logical operations
(see
(
Hartmanis and Simon 1974
;
Simon and Szegedy 1992
;
Grandjean and Jachiet 2022
)
).

In this paper, we consider the
combined complexity
of
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
and
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
, i.e. the complexity is measured in the size of both the query and the database instance.
For a
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
query
\varphi
, we define
|\varphi|
to be the sum of the arity of all factors in
\varphi
.
For a database
\mathcal{D}
, we define
|\mathcal{D}|
as sum of the size of all relations in
\mathcal{D}
.

Ackermann function.
The Ackermann function
A(m,n)
for integers
m,n\geq 0
is recursively defined as follows:

(1)

A(0,n)=n+1
;

(2)

A(m+1,0)=A(m,1)
; and

(3)

A(m+1,n+1)=A(m,A(m+1,n))
.

It is known that the Ackermann function grows faster than any primitive recursive function and therefore is not itself primitive recursive.
The inverse Ackermann function
\alpha(m,n)
is defined as

\alpha(m,n)=\min\{i\geq 1:A(i,\lfloor\frac{m}{n}\rfloor)\geq\log_{2}(n)\}

and
\alpha(m,n)
grows very slowly. For example, we have
\alpha(n,n)<5
for any practical integer
n
.

3.
Signed Acyclicity

Before we introduce the definition of signed acyclicity, we first go over the notions of
\alpha
- and
\beta
-acyclicity.

A hypergraph
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E})
is
\alpha
-acyclic if there is a tree
\mathcal{T}=(V(\mathcal{T}),E(\mathcal{T}))
and a bijective function
\chi:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow V(\mathcal{T})
such that for every vertex
v\in[n]
, the set of nodes
\{\chi(K)\mid v\in K,K\in\mathcal{E}\}
induces a connected component in
\mathcal{T}
.
A vertex
v\in[n]
is an
\alpha
-leaf of
\mathcal{H}
if the multiset
\{K\in\mathcal{E}\mid v\in K\}
contains a maximal element
K_{v}
with respect to
\subseteq
. It is known that every
\alpha
-acyclic hypergraph has an
\alpha
-leaf
(
Brault-Baron 2016
)
.

A hypergraph
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E})
is
\beta
-acyclic if for any subset
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
is
\alpha
-acyclic.
A vertex
v\in[n]
is a
\beta
-leaf of
\mathcal{H}
if the multiset
\{K\in\mathcal{E}\mid x\in K\}
can be linearly ordered by
\subseteq
. It is known that every
\beta
-acyclic hypergraph has a
\beta
-leaf
(
Brault-Baron 2016
;
Brouwer and Kolen 1980
)
.

We can now introduce the notion of signed acyclicity, slightly modified from
(
Brault-Baron 2013
)
to take multisets into account.

Definition 3.1 (Signed Acyclicity).

A signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
is
signed-acyclic
if
([n],\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
is
\alpha
-acyclic for every multiset
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}
.

Similar to
\alpha
and
\beta
-leaves, we can define
signed
-leaves.

Definition 3.2 (
signed
-leaf).

Let
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
be a signed hypergraph. We say that
x\in[n]
is a
signed
-leaf
if there exists a hyperedge
U\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
, called the pivot of
x
, such that:

(1)

(
\alpha
-property)
K\subseteq U
for every
K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
that contains
x
; and

(2)

(
\beta
-property) the multiset
\{N\in\mathcal{E}^{-}\mid x\in N,N\not\subseteq U\}\cup\{U\}
can be linearly ordered by
\subseteq
with
U
being the minimal element.

The notion of a
signed
-leaf degenerates to an
\alpha
-leaf when
\mathcal{E}^{-}=\emptyset
(since the
\beta
-property holds trivially) and reduces to a
\beta
-leaf when
\mathcal{E}^{+}
contains only singleton hyperedges (since the
\alpha
-property holds trivially and the pivot is a singleton set).
Recall that every
\alpha
-acyclic hypergraph has an
\alpha
-leaf and every
\beta
-acyclic hypergraph has a
\beta
-leaf.
The next proposition should not be surprising.

Proposition 3.3.

Every signed-acyclic signed hypergraph has a
signed
-leaf.

Next, we generalize the notion of an elimination sequence
(
Khamis et al. 2016
)
to signed hypergraphs.
Given
\mathcal{H}
and a
signed
-leaf
v
with a pivot
U
, we define
\langle{\mathcal{H}},{v}\rangle
to be the hypergraph that
(i)
removes from
\mathcal{H}
any (positive or negative) hyperedge
V
that contains
v
such that
V\subseteq U
, and
(ii)
removes
v
from any hyperedge that contains it.

Definition 3.4 (
signed
-elimination sequence).

Let
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
be a signed hypergraph. A vertex ordering
\sigma=(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n})
of all vertices in
[n]
is a
signed
-elimination sequence of
\mathcal{H}
if
v_{i}
is a
signed
-leaf of
\mathcal{H}_{i}
for every
i\in[n]
, where
\mathcal{H}_{n}=\mathcal{H}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{j}=\langle{\mathcal{H}_{j+1}},{v_{j+1}}\rangle
for
j=n-1,n-2,\dots,1
.

For a vertex ordering
\sigma=(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n-1},v_{n})
, we often denote
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot v_{n}
where
\sigma^{\prime}=(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n-1})
.
By definition, if
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot v
is a signed-elimination sequence of a signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
, then
\sigma^{\prime}
is a signed-elimination sequence of
\langle{\mathcal{H}},{v}\rangle
.

Proposition 3.5.

Let
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
be a signed hypergraph. If
\mathcal{H}
is signed-acyclic, then

(1)

\mathcal{H}
has a
signed
-elimination sequence; and

(2)

for every hyperedge
\{u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{k}\}\in\mathcal{E}^{-}
,
\mathcal{H}
has a
signed
-elimination sequence of the form
(u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{k},v_{k+1},\dots,v_{n}).

Example 3.6.

Consider the following
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
, which will function as our running example.

\displaystyle Q(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})

\displaystyle\leftarrow A(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\land U(x_{3},x_{4})\land

\displaystyle\lnot V(x_{4})\land\lnot R(x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\land\lnot S(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})

The vertex 4 is a
signed
-leaf for the hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
of
Q
with pivot the hyperedge
\{3,4\}
that corresponds to the atom
U
.
The query corresponding to the resulting hypergraph
\langle{\mathcal{H}},{4}\rangle
is:

Q^{\prime}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=A(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\land U(x_{3})\land\lnot R(x_{2},x_{3})\land\lnot S(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})

In fact,
Q
is signed-acyclic with the
signed
-elimination sequence
\sigma=(1,2,3,4)
.

We should remark here that our notion of
signed
-leaf is equivalent to the notion of a leaf for signed hypergraphs as defined in
(
Brault-Baron 2013
)
(we show this in
Appendix A
). However, for our purposes we need to define a slightly different elimination sequence, since the hypergraph after the removal of a vertex
x
is defined differently.

4.
Enumeration of full
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}

In this section, we present an algorithm that can enumerate the answers of a signed-acyclic full
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
(where
F=[n]
) with constant delay after linear preprocessing time.
The correctness and runtime analysis of the algorithm is included in Appendix
B
.

Let
Q
be a full signed-acyclic
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
with a signed-acyclic signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
and
\mathcal{D}
a database instance.
Let
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot v
be a
signed
-elimination sequence of
\mathcal{H}
.

Our preprocessing phase recursively eliminates a variable
v
in the
signed
-elimination sequence.
When eliminating
v
, the key idea is to construct in linear time a database
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, and reduce the problem of computing the answers of
Q(\mathcal{D})
to computing the answers of
Q^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
, where the signed hypergraph of
Q^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\langle{\mathcal{H}},{v}\rangle
and has a
signed
-elimination sequence
\sigma^{\prime}
.
The reduction needs to ensure that
\Pi_{[n]\setminus\{v\}}Q(\mathcal{D})=Q^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
.

4
4

4

For a tuple
\mathbf{a}_{N}
and
X\subseteq N
, we denote
\Pi_{X}\mathbf{a}_{N}
as the projection of
\mathbf{a}_{N}
on
X
.

To achieve constant delay enumeration, we construct (also in linear time) a data structure over the domain of the variable
x_{v}
, that can, given an answer to
Q^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
, extend it to an answer to
Q(\mathcal{D})
with constant delay.

Example 4.1.

We will continue with the query
Q
in Example
3.6
, the database
\mathcal{D}
in Figure
1
(a) and a
signed
-elimination sequence
\sigma=(1,2,3,4)
. The result
Q(\mathcal{D})
is depicted in Figure
1
(d).
After eliminating
x_{4}
, we obtain
Q^{\prime}
and the instance
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
in Figure
1
(b).
We have that
Q^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})=\{(\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{c}_{1}),(\mathsf{a}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{2}),(\mathsf{a}_{3},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{3})\}
.
The preprocessing phase reduces computing
Q(\mathcal{D})
to
Q^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
and produces a data structure as shown in Figure
1
(c), such that given any tuple
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
, we may use the data structure to enumerate all answers of the form
(\mathbf{a}^{\prime},a_{4})
in

4.1.
Preprocessing phase

Algorithm
1
describes the preprocessing phase that takes as input the signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
, a database instance
\mathcal{D}
, and a signed elimination sequence
\sigma
of
\mathcal{H}
.
Let
U
be a pivot hyperedge of the
signed
-leaf
v
in
\mathcal{H}
and
N_{0}=\{v\}\subseteq U\subseteq N_{1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq N_{m}
the linear order of the negative hyperedges that contain
v
.
The preprocessing phase outputs a data structure
\mathcal{L}_{v}
that maps tuples of the form
\Pi_{U\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{U}
to a doubly linked list of elements from the domain of
x_{v}
, with some extra tuple-labeled skipping links that we will introduce later.
We explain how the algorithm eliminates the variable
x_{v}
in two steps:
\alpha
-step and
\beta
-step.

Input:

signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
, instance
\mathcal{D}
,
signed
-elimination sequence
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot v

Construct :

\mathcal{L}_{v}
for every vertex
v
in
\sigma

if

\sigma
is empty

then

1

terminate

U\leftarrow
a pivot hyperedge for the
signed
-leaf
v
in
\mathcal{H}

foreach

K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{-}
such that
v\in K\subseteq U

do

\triangleright

\alpha
-step

2

if

K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}

then

3

R_{U}\leftarrow R_{U}\ltimes R_{K}

else

4

R_{U}\leftarrow R_{U}\setminus(R_{U}\ltimes R_{K})

remove
relation
R_{K}
from
\mathcal{D}

\mathcal{L}_{v}\leftarrow\mathsf{BuildList}(v,{U})

replace

R_{U}
with
R_{U\setminus\{v\}}\leftarrow\Pi_{U\setminus\{v\}}R_{U}

Let
N_{1},\dots,N_{m}\in\mathcal{E}^{-}
s.t.
U\subseteq N_{1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq N_{m}

\triangleright

\beta
-step

foreach

i=1,2,\dots,m

do

5

\mathsf{ExtendList}(\mathcal{L}_{v},N_{i})

replace

R_{N_{i}}
with

that contains all
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}\in\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}R_{N_{i}}
such that
\Pi_{U\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}\in\mathcal{L}_{v}

\text{ and }\mathcal{L}_{v}[\Pi_{U\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}].\mathsf{nextM}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}})=\bot

\mathsf{PreprocessFullCQ}(\langle{\mathcal{H}},{v}\rangle,\mathcal{D},\sigma^{\prime})

Algorithm 1

\mathsf{PreprocessFullCQ}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{D},\sigma)

Input:

a tuple
\mathbf{a}_{W}
such that
(U\setminus\{v\})\subseteq W

\mathsf{currNode}\leftarrow\mathcal{L}_{v}[\Pi_{U\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{W}]

while

\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{nextM}(\Pi_{W\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{W})\neq\bot

do

1

\mathsf{currNode}\leftarrow\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{nextM}(\Pi_{W\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{W})

emit

\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{val}

emit

\bot

Algorithm 2

\mathcal{L}_{v}.\mathsf{Iterate}(\mathbf{a}_{W})

\alpha
-step.

The algorithm first performs an
\alpha
-step (that mimics Yannakakis’ algorithm) in which we simply remove tuples from
R_{U}
that will not contribute to a query answer of
Q(\mathcal{D})
by filtering
R_{U}
with any positive (or negative) atom
R_{K}
with
v\in K
and
K\subseteq U
.

Then, Algorithm
7
builds a hash table
\mathcal{L}_{v}
that maps each tuple
\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{v\}}\in\Pi_{U\setminus\{v\}}R_{U}
to a doubly linked list of the set
\{a_{v}\mid(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{v\}},a_{v})\in R_{U}\}
,
with a slight modification that every pointer is now parameterized/labeled with
\emptyset
(see Algorithm
7
in the appendix).
The extra label on the pointer allows us to add pointers with different labels in later steps.
If
Q
contains no negative atoms, this data structure is sufficient to achieve constant delay enumeration by traversing the list from the head to the end.
Finally,
R_{U}
is replaced with its projection
\Pi_{U\setminus\{v\}}R_{U}
.

Example 4.2.

For our running example
U(x_{3},x_{4})
is chosen as the pivot, and the tuples highlighted in gray in Figure
1
(a) denotes the tuples in
U
that survive the semijoin step with the negative atom
\lnot V(x_{4})
. The linked lists of the hash table correspond to the data structure in Figure
1
(c) if we ignore the dotted edges.

\beta
-step.

Assume
\mathbf{a}\in Q^{+}(\mathcal{D})
, where
Q^{+}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}}R_{K}(\mathbf{x}_{K})
.
When negative atoms are present, for
\mathbf{a}
to be an answer to
Q(\mathcal{D})
, we also need that
\Pi_{N_{i}}\mathbf{a}\notin R_{N_{i}}
for every
i\in[m]
.
Therefore, we need to augment
\mathcal{L}_{v}
such that we can skip the enumeration of any value in the linked list that does not contribute to the answer.
The linear order on the schemas of negative atoms allows us to construct this data structure in a dynamic fashion.
If only one negative atom
\lnot R_{N_{1}}
is present (i.e.,
m=1
), for each tuple
\mathbf{a}_{N_{1}}\in R_{N_{1}}
we can add a
skipping link
labeled with
\Pi_{N_{1}\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{1}}
that bypasses the node with value
\Pi_{\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{1}}
in the doubly linked list
\mathcal{L}_{v}[\Pi_{U\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{1}}]
.
If
m=2
, then there is another negative atom
\lnot R_{N_{2}}
with
N_{1}\subseteq N_{2}
.
For each tuple
\mathbf{a}_{N_{2}}\in R_{N_{2}}
, we only need to add a skipping link labeled with
\Pi_{N_{2}\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{2}}
that bypasses the node with value
\Pi_{\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{2}}
in the doubly linked list
\mathcal{L}_{v}[\Pi_{U\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{2}}]
if that node has not been bypassed by a skipping link with label
\Pi_{N_{1}\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{2}}
yet (i.e.,
\Pi_{N_{1}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{2}}\notin R_{N_{1}}
).
In general, for every tuple
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}}\in R_{N_{i}}
such that
\Pi_{\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}}
has not been bypassed by any skipping link labeled with
\Pi_{N_{j}\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}}
(i.e., for every
1\leq j<i\leq m
,
\mathbf{a}_{N_{j}}\notin R_{N_{j}}
),
we add a skipping link labeled with
\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}}
that bypasses
\Pi_{\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}}
, as well as every skipping links labeled with
\Pi_{N_{j}\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}}
with
1\leq j<i
starting in
\Pi_{\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}}
.

Algorithm
3
implements this idea using the parameterized/labeled pointers to support the skipping links.
For any node in the doubly linked list, its next node given a tuple
\mathbf{a}_{W}
with
W\supseteq U\setminus\{v\}
is fetched by following
\mathsf{next}[\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{W}]
for the largest possible
i\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
in that node.
Recall that
N_{0}=\{v\}
and thus pointers
\mathsf{next}[\emptyset]
and
\mathsf{prev}[\emptyset]
are always present for each node.
This operation is denoted as
\mathsf{nextM}(\mathbf{a}_{W})
, and we define
\mathsf{prevM}(\mathbf{a}_{W})
similarly
(formally defined in Appendix
B
)
.
To traverse
\mathcal{L}_{v}
, we implement an iterator (Algorithm
2
) that takes a tuple
\mathbf{a}_{W}
with
W\supseteq U\setminus\{v\}
, and then traverses the linked list
\mathcal{L}_{v}[\Pi_{U\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{W}]
using
\mathsf{nextM}(\mathbf{a}_{W})
.

Input:

a data structure
\mathcal{L}_{v}
,
N_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{-}

Global variables :

U\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
,
N_{1},N_{2}\dots,N_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{-}
s.t.
v\in U
,
U\subseteq N_{1}\subseteq N_{2}\subseteq\dots\subseteq N_{i}

foreach

\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}}\in R_{N_{i}}

do

1

if

\Pi_{U}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}}\notin R_{U}
or
\exists j\in\{1,2,\dots,i-1\}
such that
\Pi_{N_{j}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}}\in R_{N_{j}}

then

2

continue

\mathsf{currNode}\leftarrow v\in\mathcal{L}_{v}[\Pi_{U\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}}]
with
v.\mathsf{val}=\Pi_{\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}}

\mathsf{prevNode}\leftarrow\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{prevM}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}})

\mathsf{nextNode}\leftarrow\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{nextM}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}})

\mathsf{prevNode}.\mathsf{next}[\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}]\leftarrow\mathsf{nextNode}

if

\mathsf{nextNode}\neq\bot

then

3

\mathsf{nextNode}.\mathsf{prev}[\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}]\leftarrow\mathsf{prevNode}

Algorithm 3

\mathsf{ExtendList}(\mathcal{L}_{v},{N_{i}})

Example 4.3.

Algorithm
3
takes as input the basic linked list data structure and all tuples in the negative atoms
\lnot R(x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
and
\lnot S(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
in Figure
1
(a) to produce the data structure in Figure
1
(c). Note that the skipping link bypassing
\mathsf{d}_{4}
with label
(\mathsf{a}_{4},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{4})
also needs to bypass both skipping links labeled by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{3})}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{3})}
that bypass
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{d}_{2}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{d}_{5}}
respectively.

Finally, for each negative atom
\lnot R_{N_{i}}
, we need to keep precisely the subset of
\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}R_{N_{i}}
so that we can
avoid
emitting an answer to
Q^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
that cannot be extended to an answer to
Q(\mathcal{D})
.
This is done by keeping the tuple
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}\in\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}R_{N_{i}}
only if

\Pi_{U\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}\in\mathcal{L}_{v}
, but the tuple cannot be extended to any answer to
Q(\mathcal{D})
, i.e., the traversal on
\mathcal{L}_{v}
using
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}
does not lead to any value of
x_{v}
.

Example 4.4.

After removing
x_{4}
from relations
A(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
and
U(x_{3},x_{4})
, we end up with relations
A(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and
U(x_{3})
in Figure
1
(b).
The query containing only the positive atoms of
Q^{\prime}
would return
\{(\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{c}_{1}),(\mathsf{a}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{2}),(\mathsf{a}_{3},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{3}),(\mathsf{a}_{4},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{3})\}
,
but we wish to skip enumerating
(\mathsf{a}_{4},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{3})
, since it cannot be extended to an answer to
Q(\mathcal{D})
. Indeed, the traversal on the linked list
\mathcal{L}_{4}[\mathsf{c}_{3}]
will simply follow the black skipping link
(\mathsf{a}_{4},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{3})
to reach the end of the list. We achieve this by adding
(\mathsf{a}_{4},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{3})
to
S(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
so that this tuple is not returned in the recursive call.

4.2.
Enumeration phase

The enumeration phase is shown in Algorithm
4
.
It follows the reverse order of
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot v
to first recursively enumerate a tuple
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\in Q^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
, and then (with a slight abuse of notation), use every
a_{v}\in\mathcal{L}_{v}.\mathsf{Iterate}(\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{\sigma^{\prime}})
to obtain an answer
(\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{\sigma^{\prime}},a_{v})
to
Q(\mathcal{D})
. The preprocessing phase guarantees that the iterator is nonempty for every answer
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{\sigma^{\prime}}
.

Input:

a signed-elimination sequence
\sigma=(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n})

Output:

an enumeration of the query answers
Q(\mathcal{D})

foreach

a_{v_{1}}\in\mathcal{L}_{v_{1}}.\mathsf{Iterate}\big(\emptyset\big)

do

1

foreach

a_{v_{2}}\in\mathcal{L}_{v_{2}}.\mathsf{Iterate}\big((a_{v_{1}})\big)

do

2

foreach

a_{v_{3}}\in\mathcal{L}_{v_{3}}.\mathsf{Iterate}\big((a_{v_{1}},a_{v_{2}})\big)

do

3

…

foreach

a_{v_{n}}\in\mathcal{L}_{v_{n}}.\mathsf{Iterate}\big((a_{v_{1}},\dots,a_{v_{n-1}})\big)

do

4

emit

(a_{v_{1}},a_{v_{2}},\dots,a_{v_{n}})

5

Algorithm 4

\mathsf{EnumerationFullCQ}(\sigma)

5.
Enumeration of
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}

In this section, we give a high-level description of the enumeration algorithm for
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
queries as
Eq

3
that constructively proves
Theorem 1.2
. The full version of the algorithm is presented in
Appendix C
, and runs on a more general class of queries, called
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
queries, with similar notions of free-connex
signed
-acyclicity.

Let
\varphi
be a free-connex
signed
-acyclic
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
query (
3
) with free variables
F=[f]
and
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
be its associated
signed
-acyclic signed hypergraph. Assume w.l.o.g that
\mathcal{H}
accepts a
signed
-elimination sequence
\sigma=(1,2,\dots,f\dots,n)
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a database instance of
\varphi
. In the preprocessing phase, the algorithm executes a sequence of
signed
-elimination steps on the
signed
-leaf
i+1
, for each
i=n-1,\dots,f
(in order). A
signed
-elimination step returns an intermediate tuple
(\varphi_{i},\mathcal{D}_{i})
, where

•

\varphi_{i}
is a
signed
-acyclic
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
query with free variables
[i]
associated with
\mathcal{H}_{i}=\langle{\mathcal{H}_{i+1}},{i+1}\rangle
(
\mathcal{H}_{n-1}=\langle{\mathcal{H}},{n}\rangle
)

•

\mathcal{D}_{i}
is an instance of
\varphi_{i}
such that
\varphi_{i}(\mathcal{D}_{i})=\varphi_{i+1}(\mathcal{D}_{i+1})
.

The sequence ends up with a full
signed
-acyclic
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
query
\varphi_{f}
with free variables
F
and
\mathcal{D}_{f}
such that
\varphi(\mathcal{D})=\varphi_{f}(\mathcal{D}_{f})
. Next, for a full
signed
-acyclic
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
query, we show in Appendix
C.1
a reduction from the enumeration problem
\mathsf{Enum}(\varphi_{f},\mathcal{D}_{f})
into
\mathsf{Enum}(Q^{*}_{f},\mathcal{D}^{*}_{f})
, where
Q^{*}_{f}
is a full
signed
-acyclic
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
and
\mathcal{D}^{*}_{f}
is a database instance of
Q^{*}_{f}
. At this point, we simply apply the preprocessing algorithm for a full
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
. In the enumeration phase, as we emit each answer
\mathbf{a}_{F}
of
Q^{*}_{f}(\mathcal{D}_{f})
, we plug the emitted tuple
\mathbf{a}_{F}
into
\varphi_{f}
to recover its weight
\varphi_{f}(\mathbf{a}_{F})=\varphi(\mathbf{a}_{F})
.

We now take a closer look at a
signed
-elimination step via a concrete running example. The formal (and rather technical) description is deferred to Appendix
C.2
up to Appendix
C.6
.

Example 5.1.

Consider the following
signed
-acyclic
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
query
\varphi(x_{1},x_{2})
, where 3 is a
signed
-leaf of its associated hypergraph with a pivot hyperedge
\{3\}
(corresponding to the positive factor
R_{3}
):

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{x_{3}}R_{1}(x_{1})\otimes R_{2}(x_{2})\otimes R_{3}(x_{3})\otimes\overline{R}_{123}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\otimes\overline{R}_{23}(x_{2},x_{3})

{\otimes}
\vdash
\vdash
R_{1}
R_{2}
R_{3}
R_{123}
\mathbf{c}_{123}
R_{23}
\mathbf{c}_{23}

\otimes
\vdash
\vdash
R_{1}
R_{2}
\mathbf{c}_{123}
R_{3}
R^{\prime}_{123}
{\otimes}
R_{23}
\mathbf{c}_{23}

\otimes
\vdash
\vdash
R_{1}
R_{2}
\otimes
R^{\prime}_{23}
R^{\prime}_{123}
\otimes
R^{\prime}_{3}

Figure 2.
The refactor steps on
\mathsf{AST}
of
\varphi
for the
signed
-leaf 3, recursively from left to right. The shaded
{\otimes}
nodes indicate the subtrees to be recursively refactored. The left is the original
\mathsf{AST}
representation of
\varphi
, and the right is its refactored
\mathsf{AST}
.

The
signed
-elimination step for the
signed
-leaf 3 is much more involved than the elimination step for a
signed
-acyclic
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
query. A naive attempt may aim for a reduction to the follwing
\varphi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=R_{1}(x_{1})\otimes R_{2}(x_{2})\otimes\overline{R^{\prime}}_{12}(x_{1},x_{2})\otimes\overline{R^{\prime}}_{2}(x_{2}),

asking for
\overline{R^{\prime}}_{12}(x_{1},x_{2})\otimes\overline{R^{\prime}}_{2}(x_{2})=\bigoplus_{x_{3}}R_{3}(x_{3})\otimes\overline{R}_{123}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\otimes\overline{R}_{23}(x_{2},x_{3})
. However, it is not attainable by the semantics of
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
queries over a general semiring. Indeed, we examine the weight
\overline{R^{\prime}}_{2}(a_{2})
, for some
x_{2}=a_{2}
such that
\Pi_{x_{3}}\overline{R}_{23}(a_{2},x_{3})\neq\emptyset
: if
x_{1}=a_{1}
with
\Pi_{x_{1},x_{2}}\overline{R}_{123}(a_{1},a_{2},x_{3})=\emptyset
, then it should encode
\bigoplus_{x_{3}}R_{3}(x_{3})\otimes\mathbf{c}_{123}\otimes\overline{R}_{23}(a_{2},x_{3})
, where
\mathbf{c}_{123}
is the constant value for entries out of the table of
\overline{R}_{123}
; otherwise, we want
\overline{R^{\prime}}_{2}(a_{2})=\mathbf{1}
and
\overline{R^{\prime}}_{12}(a_{1},a_{2})
to store
\bigoplus_{x_{3}}R_{3}(x_{3})\otimes\overline{R}_{123}(a_{1},a_{2},x_{3})\otimes\overline{R}_{23}(a_{2},x_{3})
. The two weights may not coincide over an arbitrary semiring.

This insufficiency calls for lifting
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
queries to a more expressive class of queries
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
, where we exhibit
\varphi
as an
abstract syntax tree
(
\mathsf{AST}
) depicted in Figure
2
(left), in which we decompose the negative factors as

\displaystyle\overline{R}_{123}=R_{123}\oplus\mathbbm{1}_{{\neg R_{123}}}\otimes\mathbf{c}_{123},\qquad\overline{R}_{23}=R_{23}\oplus\mathbbm{1}_{{\neg R_{23}}}\otimes\mathbf{c}_{23}

where
\mathbbm{1}_{{\neg R_{K}}}
is an indicator factor that maps to
\mathbf{0}
if
\mathbf{x}_{K}\in R_{K}
and
\mathbf{1}
otherwise. As a shorthand, we write
x\vdash y:=x\oplus\mathbbm{1}_{{\neg x}}\otimes y
. Now we illustrate the
signed
-elimination step on the
signed
-leaf 3 (corresponding to the variable
x_{3}
) in Example
5.1
. A
signed
-elimination step can be further decomposed into three steps:
(i)
refactor step,
(ii)
oracle-construction step, and
(iii)
aggregation step.

5.1.
The Refactor Step

We first illustrate the refactor step: it reorganizes the
\mathsf{AST}
of
\varphi
to make
x_{3}
present only in one subtree rooted at a child of the root
\otimes
node. First, it replaces
R_{123}
with a new factor
R^{\prime}_{123}:=R_{123}\otimes(R_{23}\vdash\mathbf{c}_{23})\otimes R_{3}
, the entry table of which is exactly that of
R_{123}
, except for multiplying the extra weight
(R_{23}\vdash\mathbf{c}_{23})\otimes R_{3}
to each table entry. Then, we get a new
\mathsf{AST}
in Figure
2
(middle) since

\displaystyle(R_{123}\vdash\mathbf{c}_{123})\otimes(R_{23}\vdash\mathbf{c}_{23})\otimes R_{3}=R^{\prime}_{123}\vdash\left((R_{23}\vdash\mathbf{c}_{23})\otimes R_{3}\otimes\mathbf{c}_{123}\right).

We keep recursing on the subtree for
(R_{23}\vdash\mathbf{c}_{23})\otimes R_{3}\otimes\mathbf{c}_{123}
, thus leading to the desired
\mathsf{AST}
in Figure
2
(right) with new factors
R^{\prime}_{23}:=R_{23}\otimes(R_{3}\vdash\mathbf{c}_{3})
and
R^{\prime}_{3}:=R_{3}\otimes\mathbf{c}_{123}\otimes\mathbf{c}_{23}
computed. Indeed,

\displaystyle(R_{23}\vdash\mathbf{c}_{23})\otimes R_{3}\otimes\mathbf{c}_{123}=R^{\prime}_{23}\vdash\left(R_{3}\otimes\mathbf{c}_{123}\otimes\mathbf{c}_{23}\right)=R^{\prime}_{23}\vdash R^{\prime}_{3}.

As we go,
R_{3}
sinks down and the linear inclusion
[3]\supseteq\{2,3\}\supseteq\{3\}
surfaces along the subtrees that contain
x_{3}
. In the end,
R_{3}
absorbs the constants
\mathbf{c}_{123}
and
\mathbf{c}_{23}
. The refactor step runs in linear time with no query (or database) size blow-up, as proven in Appendix
C.3
.

5.2.
The Oracle-construction Step

Next, we illustrate the oracle-construction step. Chazelle and Rosenberg
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1989
)
showed that a semigroup
RangeSum
data structure (oracle) on an array of size
w
can be constructed in time
O(w)
to support a semigroup sum over any range in
O(\alpha(14w,w))
time, where
\alpha
is the inverse Ackermann function. The oracle-construction step uses it as a black-box and builds a
RangeSum
oracle bottom-up for each subtree containing
x_{3}
, i.e. the subtree rooted at
\otimes
nodes in Figure
2
(right). We demonstrate via a simple database instance.

Example 5.2.

We assume the counting ring and on the refactored tree in Figure
2
(right), let
R^{\prime}_{3}
store
\{\langle i,1\rangle,i\in[15]\}
,
R^{\prime}_{23}
store
\{\langle(\mathsf{a}_{1},3),2\rangle,\langle(\mathsf{a}_{1},9),2\rangle,\langle(\mathsf{a}_{2},6),3\rangle,\langle(\mathsf{a}_{2},11),3\rangle,\langle(\mathsf{a}_{3},8),4\rangle\}

and

R^{\prime}_{123}
store
\langle(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{1},4),5\rangle,\langle(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{1},15),5\rangle,\langle(\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{a}_{1},3),6\rangle
,
\langle(\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{a}_{2},12),6\rangle
.

The oracle-construction step starts from an array representation of the database instance drawn in Figure
3
, indexed by all possible values of
x_{3}
. As an example, the tuple
\langle(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{1},4),5\rangle
of
R^{\prime}_{123}
can be accessed as the
4
-th element of the array
R^{\prime}_{123}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})
. The necessity of
RangeSum
becomes natural: after factoring out the term
R_{1}(\mathsf{b}_{1})\otimes R_{2}(\mathsf{a}_{1})
(independent of
x_{3}
), the aggregation for
x_{1}=\mathsf{b}_{1},x_{2}=\mathsf{a}_{1}
, from the lens of the array
R^{\prime}_{123}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})
, is

(4)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{x_{3}}R^{\prime}_{123}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})\vdash(R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})\vdash R^{\prime}_{3}(x_{3}))=\sum_{1\leq x_{3}\leq 2}1+

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{3}=3}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}+\sum_{x_{3}=4}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0}5}+\sum_{5\leq x_{3}\leq 8}1+\sum_{x_{3}=9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}+\sum_{10\leq x_{3}\leq 14}1+\sum_{x_{3}=15}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0}5}

where each term is a sum over a range. To support fast aggregation, we construct a
RangeSum
oracle on the array of
R^{\prime}_{3}(x_{3})
. Then, we build a
RangeSum
oracle on the array of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{2},x_{3})}
. In particular, we query the oracle of
R^{\prime}_{3}(x_{3})
to fill in the partial sums (as in the 3rd, 4th row in Figure
3
) and then construct
RangeSum
oracles over the partial sums.
Take
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})}
as the example,
[5,8]:\textbf{4}
denotes querying the oracle
R^{\prime}_{3}(x_{3})
with
5\leq x_{3}\leq 8
as range and getting back the value
4
. Then the
RangeSum
oracle for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})}
is constructed over the array (of partial sums):

\displaystyle[1,2]:\textbf{2}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textbf{2}}\quad[4,4]:\textbf{1}\quad[5,8]:\textbf{4}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textbf{2}}\quad[10,14]:\textbf{5}.\quad[15,15]:\textbf{1}

where the ranges above split at
x_{3}=3,9
and
x_{3}=4,15
in account for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0}R^{\prime}_{123}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})}
, respectively. The rationale here is to avoid mis-aligned ranges when querying the oracle of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})}
during the oracle-construction of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0}R^{\prime}_{123}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})}
. The details are in the 6th row of Figure
3
. The oracle-construction of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{2},x_{3})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R^{\prime}_{123}(\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{a}_{2},x_{3})}
are similarly depicted in the 4th and 7th row of Figure
3
. It is worth noting that the splits of ranges should be choreographed for query range alignment, and yet can not be arbitrarily fine-grained due to possible blow-ups in time and space. Indeed, we formally show in Appendix
C.5
that desired oracles can be carefully constructed in linear time and space.

Figure 3.
The oracle-construction steps on the refactored
\mathsf{AST}
. The oracle of
R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})
,
R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{2},x_{3})
and
R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{3},x_{3})
are built atop the oracle of
R^{\prime}_{3}(x_{3})
; the oracle of
R^{\prime}_{123}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})
and
R^{\prime}_{123}(\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{a}_{2},x_{3})
are built atop the oracle of
R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})
and

, respectively. The leftmost column shows the requested range sums over
[1,15]
in the aggregation step.

5.3.
The Aggregation Step

Finally, we go over the aggregation step that effectively peels off
x_{3}
from the refactored
\mathsf{AST}
. To that end, it pushes the aggregation operator
\bigoplus_{x_{3}}
downward the refactored
\mathsf{AST}
from root to
R^{\prime}_{3}(x_{3})
, i.e. the opposite direction of the oracle-construction step. Start with the root
\otimes
node, we will construct new factors
R^{\prime\prime}_{12}(x_{1},x_{2})
and
R^{\prime\prime}_{2}(x_{2})
such that (let
\bigoplus_{x_{3}}R^{\prime}_{3}=15
):

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{x_{3}}R^{\prime}_{123}

\displaystyle\vdash(R^{\prime}_{23}\vdash R^{\prime}_{3})=R^{\prime\prime}_{12}\vdash\bigoplus_{x_{3}}(R^{\prime}_{23}\vdash R^{\prime}_{3})

\displaystyle=R^{\prime\prime}_{12}\vdash(R^{\prime\prime}_{2}\vdash\bigoplus_{x_{3}}R^{\prime}_{3})=R^{\prime\prime}_{12}\vdash(R^{\prime\prime}_{2}\vdash 15).

To realize the first equality, we ask the table of
R^{\prime\prime}_{12}(x_{1},x_{2})
to store two entries,
\langle(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{1}),25\rangle
and
\langle(\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{a}_{2}),29\rangle
, where
25
is the aggregation for
x_{1}=\mathsf{b}_{1},x_{2}=\mathsf{a}_{1}
in (
4
), that can be obtained by a simple range query
[1,15]
on the oracle of
R^{\prime}_{123}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{1},x_{3})
. Similarly for
29
from a
[1,15]
query on the oracle of
R^{\prime}_{123}(\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{a}_{2},x_{3})
. Indeed, the first equality holds because for all values of
(x_{1},x_{2})
not encoded in the table of
R^{\prime\prime}_{12}
, we always have
R^{\prime}_{123}=\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbbm{1}_{{\neg R^{\prime}_{123}}}=\mathbf{1}
, therefore for those
(x_{1},x_{2})
s,
R^{\prime}_{123}\vdash(R^{\prime}_{23}\vdash R^{\prime}_{3})=\mathbf{0}\oplus\mathbf{1}\otimes(R^{\prime}_{23}\vdash R^{\prime}_{3})=R^{\prime}_{23}\vdash R^{\prime}_{3}
.

Now that we have pushed
\bigoplus_{x_{3}}
to
(R^{\prime}_{23}\vdash R^{\prime}_{3})
(i.e. the
\otimes
node on the 2nd level of the refactored
\mathsf{AST}
) and we insert a single tuple
\langle(\mathsf{a}_{3}),18\rangle
into the new factor
R^{\prime\prime}_{2}(x_{2})
, where
18
is yet another
[1,15]
query on the oracle of
R^{\prime}_{23}(\mathsf{a}_{3},x_{3})
. The second equality holds because for all values of
x_{2}
not encoded in the table of
R^{\prime\prime}_{2}
, we have
R^{\prime}_{23}=\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbbm{1}_{{\neg R^{\prime}_{23}}}=\mathbf{1}
. Thus,
R^{\prime}_{23}\vdash R^{\prime}_{3}=\mathbf{0}\oplus\mathbf{1}\otimes R^{\prime}_{3}=R^{\prime}_{3}
. The resulting
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
query
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
after the
signed
-elimination on
3
is

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime\prime}(x_{1},x_{2})=R_{1}(x_{1})\otimes R_{2}(x_{2})\otimes\left(R^{\prime\prime}_{12}(x_{1},x_{2})\vdash(R^{\prime\prime}_{2}(x_{2})\vdash 15)\right)

where the
\mathsf{AST}
of
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
is exactly the refactored
\mathsf{AST}
in Figure
2
(right), except that
x_{3}
is being eliminated.
Now the
signed
-elimination step is complete. We defer the formal analysis of the aggregation step to Appendix
C.4
and Appendix
C.6
.

5.4.
The Reduction to Enumeration of Full
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}

Finally, we cast the enumeration of the (full)
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
query
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
into the enumeration of the full
signed
-acyclic
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
query
Q^{*}
, by directly extracting factors from
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
as relations:

\displaystyle Q^{*}(x_{1},x_{2})\leftarrow R^{*}_{1}(x_{1})\wedge R^{*}_{2}(x_{2})\wedge\neg R^{**}_{12}(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge\neg R^{**}_{2}(x_{2})

where
R^{*}_{1}
stores
a_{1}\in R_{1}
with
R_{1}(a_{1})\neq\mathbf{0}
(similarly for
R^{*}_{2}
), and
R^{**}_{12}
stores
(a_{1},a_{2})\in R^{\prime\prime}_{12}
with
R^{\prime\prime}_{12}(a_{1},a_{2})=\mathbf{0}
(similarly for
R^{**}_{2}
). Intuitively,
Q^{*}
forbids exactly the tuples
(a_{1},a_{2})
such that
\varphi^{\prime\prime}(x_{1},x_{2})=\mathbf{0}
to be emitted. Thus we can safely enumerate answers of
Q^{*}
using techniques in Section
4
and use
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
to recover the non-
\mathbf{0}
weights.

6.
Lower Bounds

In this section, we present conditional and unconditional lower bounds which complement our upper bounds. All lower bounds here refer to
self-join-free
queries, which means that each relation name appears at most once in the body of the query. Technical proofs of this section can be found in Appendix
D
.

6.1.
Lower Bounds for
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}

In this section, we show lower bound results for any
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
that is not free-connex
signed
-acyclic. We split this result into two theorems that use different conditional lower bounds.

Problem:

(k+1,k)
-Hyperclique

Input:

a
k
-uniform hypergraph
5
5

5

A hypergraph is said to be
k
-uniform
if every hyperedge contains exactly
k
vertices.
(for
k>2
)

Output:

does it contain a hyperclique of size
k+1
, i.e. a set of
k+1
vertices where every subset of size
k
forms a hyperedge

Conjecture 6.1 (
(k+1,k)
-Hyperclique).

There is no algorithm that solves
(k+1,k)
-Hyperclique in
O(m)
time, where
m
is the number of edges in the input hypergraph.

Readers are referred to
(
Berkholz et al. 2020
)
for evidence why Conjecture
6.1
is believable. When
k=2
, the (3,2)-Hyperclique problem is the problem of finding a triangle in a graph.

Conjecture 6.2 (Triangle).

There is no algorithm that decides whether a graph with
n
nodes contains a triangle in
O(n^{2})
time.

The following is a combination of results from
(
Brault-Baron 2013
;
Berkholz et al. 2020
)
.

Theorem 6.3.

Let
Q
be a
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
that is not
signed
-acyclic. Assuming Conjecture
6.1
and Conjecture
6.2
, then there is no algorithm for
Q
that has linear preprocessing time and
O(1)
delay.

To show a lower bound for queries that are not free-connex (but are
signed
-acyclic), we will use a weaker lower bound conjecture that implies
Triangle
.

Conjecture 6.4 (
\mathsf{BMM}
).

There is no algorithm that computes the product
A\times B
of two
n\times n
Boolean matrices
A
and
B
in
O(n^{2})
time.

Evidence for Conjecture
6.4
can be found in
(
Raz 2003
)
. Bagan et al.
(
Bagan et al. 2007
)
reduce the
BMM
problem to the non-free-connex acyclic query
Q(x,y)\leftarrow\bigvee_{z}A(x,z)\wedge B(z,y)
and apply Conjecture
6.4
to obtain a conditional lower bound. The
matrix multiplication exponent

\omega
is the smallest number such that for any
\epsilon>0
, there is an algorithm that multiplies two
n
-by-
n
matrices with at most
O(n^{\omega+\epsilon})
operations (assuming RAM model). The best bound known so-far on
\omega
is (roughly)
\omega<2.373
in
(
Williams 2012
;
Gall 2014
)
. We note that Conjecture
6.4
does not violate the common belief that
\omega=2
, since that only implies that
BMM
can be computed in time
n^{2+o(1)}
. For non-free-connex CQs, a weaker lower bound conjecture was used,
sparse BMM
(the matrices have
m
non-zero entries and no
O(m)
algorithm exists). However, this conjecture cannot be applied in our case because we need to take the complement of the matrix to populate a negated atom, and that means that a sparse matrix becomes dense.

Theorem 6.5.

Let
Q
be a
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
that is
signed
-acyclic and not free-connex. Assuming Conjecture
6.4
, there is no algorithm with linear preprocessing time and
O(1)
delay.

6.2.
Lower Bounds for
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}

We present next lower bounds for
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
when restricted to queries with head
\varphi()
, which we denote as
\mathsf{SumProd}^{\neg}
. To show these bounds, we will use weaker conjectures than the ones used in the previous section.

Conjecture 6.6 (Minimum-Weight
k
-Clique).

There is no algorithm that computes the minimum weight of a
k
-clique in a edge-weighted graph with
n
nodes in
O(n^{k})
time.

Our reduction from Minimum-Weight
k
-Clique
(
Abboud et al. 2018
)
is an application of the clique embedding power technique introduced in
(
Fan et al. 2023
)
.

Theorem 6.7.

Assuming Conjecture
6.6
, a
\mathsf{SumProd}^{\neg}

\varphi
over the tropical semiring can be solved in linear time iff
\varphi
is
signed
-acyclic.

Over the counting ring, we can instead show that any lower bound for counting Boolean CQs transfers immediately to
\mathsf{SumProd}^{\neg}
.

Theorem 6.8.

Suppose that no linear-time algorithm can count the solutions of a non
\alpha
-acyclic Boolean CQ.
Then, there is no linear-time algorithm for a
\mathsf{SumProd}^{\neg}
query over the counting ring that is not
signed
-acyclic.

6.3.
An Unconditional Lower Bound for
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}

Finally, we show an unconditional lower bound that provides some evidence on the necessity of the inverse Ackermann factor in the runtime of Theorem
1.2
.

The lower bound is based on the additive structure of the underlying semiring for
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
, i.e. a commutative semigroup with operator
\oplus
. It uses the
arithmetic model of computation

(
Yao 1985
;
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
, which charges one unit of computation for every
\oplus
operation performed, while all other computation is free. Essentially, the computation can be viewed as a sequence of instructions of the form
z_{i}=az_{j}\oplus bz_{k}
, where
\{z_{i}\}_{i}
form an unbounded set of variables. Moreover, this sequence should be agnostic to the actual values of the semiring. The only thing we need is that the semigroup is
faithful

(
Yao 1985
;
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
, meaning that for every
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
, and integers
\delta_{i},\delta_{j}^{\prime}>0
,

\bigoplus_{i\in T_{1}}\delta_{i}\cdot a_{i}=\bigoplus_{j\in T_{2}}\delta_{j}^{\prime}\cdot a_{j}

cannot be an identity for all
a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}\in S
unless
T_{1}=T_{2}
.
This is essentially saying that there is no “magical shortcut” to compute the sums.

Theorem 6.9.

Under the arithmetic model of computation, any constant delay enumeration algorithm (in data complexity) for

\varphi(x)=\bigoplus_{y}A(x)\otimes B(y)\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{{\lnot R}}(x,y)

on factors
A
,
B
each of size
n
and factor
R
of size
2m

must require
\Omega(m\cdot\alpha(m,m))
preprocessing time for every
m\leq n
.

7.
Difference of CQs

As an application of our results, we consider the class of queries of the form
Q_{1}-Q_{2}
, where
Q_{1},Q_{2}
are full CQs with the same set of variables. It is shown in a recent paper
(
Hu and Wang 2023
)
that
Q_{1}-Q_{2}
can be computed in time
O(|\mathcal{D}|+\mathsf{OUT})
, where
\mathsf{OUT}
is the output size of
Q_{1}-Q_{2}
, if
Q_{1}
is
\alpha
-acyclic and
Q_{1}\wedge R_{e}
is
\alpha
-acyclic for every
R_{e}
in
Q_{2}
. We use our main theorem to strengthen this result by providing a constant-delay enumeration guarantee (Appendix
E
).

Theorem 7.1.

Let
Q=Q_{1}-Q_{2}
, where
Q_{1},Q_{2}
are full CQs over the same set of variables. If
Q_{1}
is
\alpha
-acyclic and
Q_{1}\wedge R_{e}
is
\alpha
-acyclic for every
R_{e}
in
Q_{2}
, then the result
Q
can be enumerated with constant delay after
O(|\mathcal{D}|)
preprocessing time.

As a corollary, we obtain the following generalization to differences of non-full CQs (proven in Appendix
E
).

Corollary 7.2.

Let
Q=Q_{1}-Q_{2}
, where
Q_{1},Q_{2}
are CQs with the same set of free variables. If
Q
is difference-linear (Def 2.3 in
(
Hu and Wang 2023
)
), then the output of
Q
can be enumerated with constant delay after
O(|\mathcal{D}|)
preprocessing time.

8.
Conclusion

This paper has made an initial foray into a novel way of interpreting
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
from the perspective of semiring and
FAQ
s
(
Khamis et al. 2016
)
. We presented a constant-delay enumeration algorithm for the class of free-connex
signed
-acyclic
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
queries, after linear preprocessing (modulo an inverse Ackermann factor), and showed lower bounds for
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
queries out of this class.
We leave as an intriguing open question the parameterized complexity of general
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
and
\mathsf{FAQ}^{\neg}
queries (a brief discussion is in Appendix
F
).

Appendix A
Missing proofs in Section
3

Let
\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
be a signed hypergraph. We define
[{\mathcal{H}}]
as the hypergraph obtained by iteratively removing a hyperedge
K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{-}
if there exists some hyperedge
U\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
such that
K\subseteq U
.
We say a signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
is
reduced
if
\mathcal{H}=[{\mathcal{H}}]
.
For a multiset of hyperedges
\mathcal{E}
, we define
{\mathcal{E}}{[\setminus{x}]}:=\{K\setminus\{x\}\mid K\in\mathcal{E}\}
.
Define
{\mathcal{H}}{[\setminus{x}]}
as the hypergraph obtained by removing
x
from every hyperedge in
\mathcal{H}
.

A.1.
Equivalence with
(
Brault-Baron 2013
)

A vertex
x
is said to be a
bicolor-leaf
in
\mathcal{H}
in
(
Brault-Baron 2013
)
if
x
is a
\beta
-leaf in
[{\mathcal{H}}]
. We show that this notion of bicolor-leaf in
(
Brault-Baron 2013
)
is equivalent to our definition of
signed
-leaf in Definition
3.2
.

Lemma A.1.

Let
\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
be a signed-acyclic signed hypergraph. Then,
x
is a
signed
-leaf of
\mathcal{H}
if and only if
x
is a
\beta
-leaf of
[{\mathcal{H}}]
.

Proof.

Let
[{\mathcal{H}}]=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{+},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{-})
.

\implies
Assume that
x
is a
signed
-leaf in
\mathcal{H}
. Let
U
be the pivot edge of
x
in
\mathcal{H}
and assume that
U\subseteq R_{1}\subseteq R_{2}\subseteq\dots\subseteq R_{n}
such that
R_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{-}
for
i\in[n]
.
Then
U,R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{n}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{-}
.
Let
K
be an arbitrary hyperedge in
\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{-}\setminus\{U,R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{n}\}
that contains
x
. Then by definition of
[{\mathcal{H}}]
, we must have that
K\subseteq U
, and therefore
K
is not present in
[{\mathcal{H}}]
.
Therefore,
U,R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{n}
are all hyperedges in
[{\mathcal{H}}]
that contain
x
. Hence
x
is a
\beta
-leaf of
[{\mathcal{H}}]
.

\impliedby
Assume that
x
is a
\beta
-leaf in
[{\mathcal{H}}]
.
Let
R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{n}
be all hyperedges in
[{\mathcal{H}}]
that contain
x
, and assume that and
R_{i}\subseteq R_{i+1}
for
i\in[n-1]
.

We argue that
\{R_{1}\}=\mathcal{E}^{+}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{+}
, i.e.
R_{1}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{+}
and for every
i\in\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
,
R_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{-}
.
Let
i
be the smallest index such that
R_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{+}
.
Such an
i
must exist, since otherwise otherwise we have
x\in\bigcup_{K\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{-}}K
but
x\notin\bigcup_{K\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{+}}K
, a contradiction to
\bigcup_{K\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{-}}K\subseteq\bigcup_{K\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{+}}K
.
If
i>1
, since
R_{1}\subseteq R_{i}
, then
R_{1}
should have been removed from
[{\mathcal{H}}]
, a contradiction.
Finally, if there is any
j\in\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
such that
R_{j}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{+}
, since
R_{1}\subseteq R_{j}
,
R_{1}
would have been removed from
[{\mathcal{H}}]
, a contradiction.

Let
U=R_{1}
. We show that
U
is a pivot edge in
\mathcal{H}
.

For (1), let
R
be an arbitrary hyperedge in
\mathcal{E}^{+}
that contains
x
. Consider the maximal sequence of hyperedges
\{R^{(i)}\}_{i\geq 0}
such that
R^{(0)}=R
, and for each
i\geq 0
, if
R^{(i)}\notin\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{+}
, then by construction of
[{\mathcal{H}}]
, let
R^{(i+1)}
be the hyperedge in
\mathcal{E}^{+}
such that
R^{(i)}\subset R^{(i+1)}
.
Since the hypergraph is finite, this sequence must terminate and assume that its last hyperedge is
R^{(t)}
.
We thus have that
R^{(t)}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{+}
, and by previous argument,
R^{(t)}=R_{1}
.
Therefore,
R=R^{(0)}\subset R^{(1)}\subset\dots\subset R^{(t)}=R_{1}=U
, as desired.

Consider (2). let
N
be an arbitrary hyperedge in
\mathcal{E}^{-}
such that
x\in N
and
N\not\subseteq U
.
We show that
N\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{-}
.
Indeed, if not, we must have that there exists some hyperedge
K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
such that
N\subseteq K
. Since
K\subseteq U
, we have
N\subseteq U
, a contradiction.
Therefore, we have
\{N\in\mathcal{E}^{-}\mid x\in N,N\not\subseteq R_{x}\}\cup\{U\}=\{R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{n}\}
, which is linearly ordered by
\subseteq
by construction and
U=R_{1}
is the minimal element, as desired.
∎

A.2.
Proof of Proposition
3.3

Our proof for Proposition
3.3
is inspired from the proof of Theorem 7 in
(
Brault-Baron 2016
)
.
Some additional definitions are required.
Two vertices
x
and
y
are said to be
non-neighbors
in a signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
if there is no hyperedge
R\in\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{-}
such that
R\subset\mathcal{V}
and
x,y\in R
.

We need some additional structural properties on signed-acyclicity.

Lemma A.2.

Let
\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
be an
\alpha
-acyclic hypergraph. Then we have

(1)

the hypergraph
{\mathcal{H}}{[\setminus{x}]}
is
\alpha
-acyclic for every
vertex
x\in\mathcal{V}
; and

(2)

the hypergraph
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}\setminus\{R\})
is
\alpha
-acyclic for every two distinct hyperedges
R
and
S
in
\mathcal{H}
such that
R\subseteq S
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be the join tree of
\mathcal{H}
witnessed by the bijection mapping
\chi:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow V(\mathcal{T})
.
Consider two items.

(
1
)
We have that
{\mathcal{H}}{[\setminus{x}]}=(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\},{\mathcal{E}}{[\setminus{x}]})
.
Consider the new mapping
\chi^{\prime}:{\mathcal{E}}{[\setminus{x}]}\rightarrow V(\mathcal{T})
such that
\chi^{\prime}(K\setminus\{x\})=\chi(K)
for every
K\in\mathcal{E}
.
It is easy to verify that for every vertex
v\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}
, the set of nodes
\{\chi^{\prime}(K\setminus\{x\})\mid K\setminus\{x\}\in{\mathcal{E}}{[\setminus{x}]}\}
induces a connected component in
\mathcal{T}
since the set of nodes
\{\chi(K)\mid K\in\mathcal{E}\}
does.

(
2
)
Let
R
and
S
be two distinct hyperedges in
\mathcal{H}
such that
R\subseteq S
.
Let
\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2},\dots,\mathcal{T}_{k}
be the connected components of
\mathcal{T}-\chi(R)
, the tree obtained by removing the node
\chi(R)
and all edges incident to
\chi(R)
from
\mathcal{T}
.
Assume that
\chi(R)
is adjacent to
\chi({A_{i}})
in
\mathcal{T}_{i}
for each
i\in[k]
and without loss of generality that
\chi(S)
is contained in
\mathcal{T}_{k}
.
Consider the tree
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
obtained by removing
\chi(R)
from
\mathcal{T}
and adding an edge between every
\chi({A_{i}})
to
\chi(S)
for each
i\in[k-1]
.

We argue that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is a join tree of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
. Consider any vertex
x
in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
and let
K_{x}=\{\chi(K)\mid x\in K,K\in\mathcal{E}\setminus\{R\}\}
. The goal is to show that
K_{x}
induces a connected component in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.

Consider
I=\{i\in[k]\mid V(\mathcal{T}_{i})\cap K_{x}\neq\emptyset\}
, the indices of all subtrees
\mathcal{T}_{i}
that contains some hyperedge containing
x
.
If
I=\{i\}
for some index
i\in[k]
, then
K_{x}
is properly contained in
\mathcal{T}_{i}
, and since
\mathcal{T}_{i}
is a join tree,
\mathcal{T}_{x}
induces a connected component in
\mathcal{T}_{i}
and thus
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.
Otherwise,
|{I}|\geq 2
. In this case, we must have
A_{i}\in K_{x}
for every
i\in I
and thus
x\in R\subseteq S
.
Therefore,
K_{x}\setminus V(\mathcal{T}_{k})
induces a connected component in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. By construction, every
\chi(A_{i})
is connected to
\chi(S)
in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
and
\chi(S)\in K_{x}
. Note that
K_{x}\cap V(\mathcal{T}_{k})
also induces a connected component in
\mathcal{T}_{k}
and
\chi(S)\in K_{x}\cap V(\mathcal{T}_{k})
,
K_{x}
induces a connected component in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma A.3.

Let
\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
be a reduced signed-acyclic signed hypergraph with at least two vertices. Then
\mathcal{H}
contains two non-neighbor
\beta
-leaves (and therefore signed-leaves).

Proof.

We define the size of a signed hypergraph as the sum of the size of each hyperedge in it.
We use an induction on the size
k
of the reduced signed-acyclic signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
.

Basis
k=2
.:

In this case, we can only have
\mathcal{H}=(\{x,y\},\{\{x\},\{y\}\},\emptyset)
,
\mathcal{H}=(\{x,y\},\{\{x\},\{y\}\},\{\{x,y\}\})
or
\mathcal{H}=(\{x,y\},\{\{x,y\}\},\emptyset)
and the claim follows.

Inductive step.:

Assume that the claim holds for any reduced signed-acyclic signed hypergraph with size between
2
and
k-1
.
Let
\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
be a reduced signed-acyclic signed hypergraph with size
k
.

Assume first that there is some hyperedge
R\in\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{-}
in
\mathcal{H}
such that
R=\mathcal{V}
.
If
R\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
, since
\mathcal{H}
is reduced, we must have
\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{V},\{R\},\emptyset)
, and the lemma follows since pair of vertices in
\mathcal{H}
are non-neighbor
\beta
-leaves.
If
R\in\mathcal{E}^{-}
, consider
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-}\setminus\{R\})
. Note that
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is also signed-acyclic, reduced and of smaller size.
Thus by the inductive hypothesis,
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
contains two non-neighbor
\beta
-leaves
x
and
y
.
Then
x
and
y
are also
\beta
-leaves in
\mathcal{H}
, since
K\subseteq\mathcal{V}=R
for every hyperedge
K
in
\mathcal{H}
.
The vertices
x
and
y
are non-neighbors, because if not, there exists some hyperedge
K
in
\mathcal{H}
such that
x,y\in K
and
K\subset R
, and thus
K
is in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
, a contradiction to that
x
and
y
are non-neighbors in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}

In what follows, we assume that
\mathcal{H}
contains no hyperedge
R
with
R=\mathcal{V}
and has at least 3 vertices (by the inductive step).

Claim A.1.

If
x
is a
\alpha
-leaf in a reduced signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
, then there exists a
\beta
-leaf
y
in
\mathcal{H}
such that
x
and
y
are non-neighbors in
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

Assume that
x
is an
\alpha
-leaf in
\mathcal{H}
.
Then there exists a hyperedge
R_{x}\in\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{-}
such that for any hyperedge
S\in\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{-}
that contains
x
,
S\subseteq R_{x}
.
Note that
R_{x}\neq\mathcal{V}
.

Consider the signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=[{{\mathcal{H}}{[\setminus{x}]}}]
, i.e.,
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is obtained by first removing
x
from
\mathcal{H}
and then taking its reduced hypergraph.

By Proposition
A.6
,
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is signed-acyclic with size less than
k
.
By the inductive hypothesis,
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
contains two non-neighbor
\beta
-leaves
y
and
z
.
Since
y
and
x
are non-neighbors and
R_{x}\neq\mathcal{V}
, either
y
or
z
is not contained in
R_{x}\setminus\{x\}
and we assume that
y\notin R_{x}\setminus\{x\}
.

We argue that there is no hyperedge
S
in
\mathcal{H}
that can contain both vertices
x
and
y
.
Indeed, if not, assume that
S
contains both
x
and
y
in
\mathcal{H}
.
By definition of
R_{x}
, we have that
S\subseteq R_{x}
, and we would have
y\in R_{x}
, a contradiction to our choice of
y
.

Let
R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{n}
be all hyperedges in
\mathcal{H}
such that
y\in R_{i}
for each
i\in[n]
.
We argue that every
R_{i}
also appear in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=[{{\mathcal{H}}{[\setminus{x}]}}]
.
First, every
R_{i}
appears in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}={\mathcal{H}}{[\setminus{x}]}
since we argued that there is no hyperedge in
\mathcal{H}
that contains both
x
and
y
.
Suppose for contradiction that some hyperedge
R_{i}
is not contained in
[{{\mathcal{H}}{[\setminus{x}]}}]
.
Then there exists some hyperedge
K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
such that
R_{i}\subseteq K\setminus\{x\}\subseteq K
, a contradiction that
\mathcal{H}
is reduced.

By the inductive hypothesis,
y
is a
\beta
-leaf in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=[{{\mathcal{H}}{[\setminus{x}]}}]
, and thus
y
is a
\beta
-leaf in
\mathcal{H}
since the chain
R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{n}
in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
remains in
\mathcal{H}
.
∎

Since
\mathcal{H}
is signed-acyclic,
\mathcal{H}
is
\alpha
-acyclic and thus contains an
\alpha
-leaf
x
.
By Claim
A.1
, there is a
\beta
-leaf
y
in
\mathcal{H}
that is non-neighbor with
x
. Then
y
is also an
\alpha
-leaf in
\mathcal{H}
, and by Claim
A.1
again, there is a
\beta
-leaf
z
in
\mathcal{H}
that is non-neighbor with
y
. Hence
y
and
z
are non-neighbor
\beta
-leaves in
\mathcal{H}
, as desired.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.4.

Every signed-acyclic signed hypergraph with at least two-vertices have two non-neighbor signed-leaves.

Proof.

When
\mathcal{H}
contains at least two vertices, by Lemma
A.2
and
A.3
,
[{\mathcal{H}}]
is reduced, signed-acyclic and contains two non-neighbor
\beta
-leaves
x
and
y
.
By Lemma
A.1
,
x
and
y
are both
signed
-leaves of
\mathcal{H}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a signed-acyclic signed hypergraph. If
\mathcal{H}
contains only one vertex
x
, then it is only possible that
\mathcal{H}=(\{x\},\{\{x\}\},\mathcal{E}^{-})
where
\mathcal{E}^{-}\subseteq\{\{x\}\}
, and in all cases
x
is a
signed
-leaf of
\mathcal{H}
.
If
\mathcal{H}
contains at least two vertices, the claim follows by Lemma
A.4
.
∎

A.3.
Proof of Proposition
3.5

Corollary A.5.

Let
\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
be a signed-acyclic signed hypergraph. Then we have

(1)

the hypergraph
{\mathcal{H}}{[\setminus{x}]}
is signed-acyclic, for every
vertex
x\in\mathcal{V}
; and

(2)

let
R\in\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{-}
and
S\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
be two distinct hyperedges with
R\subseteq S
, and we have
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{+}\setminus\{R\},\mathcal{E}^{-}\setminus\{R\})
is signed-acyclic.

Proof.

We again consider two items.

(
1
)
Let
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{-}
and we have that
{\mathcal{H}}{[\setminus{x}]}=(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\},{\mathcal{E}^{+}}{[\setminus{x}]},{\mathcal{E}^{-}}{[\setminus{x}]})
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{x}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}^{-}}{[\setminus{x}]}
and consider the hypergraph
\mathcal{H}_{x}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\},{\mathcal{E}^{+}}{[\setminus{x}]}\cup\mathcal{E}_{x}^{\prime})
.
By construction, we must have some
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{x}^{\prime}={\mathcal{E}^{-}}{[\setminus{x}]}
.
Since
\mathcal{H}
is signed-acyclic, we have that the hypergraph
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
is
\alpha
-acyclic.
Then
{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}{[\setminus{x}]}=(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\},{\mathcal{E}^{+}}{[\setminus{x}]}\cup{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}{[\setminus{x}]})=(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\},{\mathcal{E}^{+}}{[\setminus{x}]}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{x})=\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{x}
is also
\alpha
-acyclic by Lemma
A.2
, as desired.

(
2
)
The claim is straightforward if
R\in\mathcal{E}^{-}
.
Assume that
R\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
.
Consider any hypergraph
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{V},(\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\setminus\{R\})
where
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}
.
Note that the hypergraph
\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
is
\alpha
-acyclic since
\mathcal{H}
is signed-acyclic.
Since
\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}
is
\alpha
-acyclic,
R\subseteq S
and
S\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
, by Lemma
A.2
,
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is
\alpha
-acyclic, as desired.
∎

Proposition A.6.

If
\mathcal{H}
is a signed-acyclic signed hypergraph and
x
is a
signed
-leaf of
\mathcal{H}
, then
\langle{\mathcal{H}},{x}\rangle
is signed-acyclic.

Proof.

Immediate from Corollary
A.5
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
be a signed-acyclic signed hypergraph.
By Proposition
A.6
and
3.3
,
\mathcal{H}
always has a
signed
-elimination sequence, and (1) follows.

For (2), let
R=\{u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{k}\}\in\mathcal{E}^{-}
. The claim follows if
R=[n]
.
Assume that
\mathcal{H}
contains at least two vertices and
R\subset[n]
.

Construct the following sequence of hypergraphs
\mathcal{H}_{n}
,
\mathcal{H}_{n-1}
,
\dots
,
\mathcal{H}_{k}
, where

•

\mathcal{H}_{n}=\mathcal{H}
;

•

for every
j=n,n-1,\dots,k+1
, let
v_{j}
be a signed-leaf in
\mathcal{H}_{j}
such that
v_{j}\notin R
, and let
\mathcal{H}_{j-1}=\langle{\mathcal{H}_{j}},{v_{j}}\rangle
.

We argue that in the second step, such a
v_{j}
must exist. For
j=n
, since
R\subset[n]
and
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is signed-acyclic, by Lemma
A.4
,
\mathcal{H}_{n}
contains two non-neighbor signed-leaves, and there must be a signed-leaf
v_{n}
of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
such that
v_{n}\notin R
.
Hence,
R\subset[n]\setminus\{v_{n}\}
and
R
remain in
\langle{\mathcal{H}_{n}},{v_{n}}\rangle
by definition.
This argument can thus continue inductively.

Note that
R
would contain every vertex in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
since
\mathcal{H}_{k}
has exactly
k
vertices. Then
\mathcal{H}_{k}
would admit a signed-elimination sequence
(u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{k})
, and thus
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{n}
admits a signed elimination sequence

(u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{k},v_{k+1},\dots,v_{n})
as desired.
∎

Appendix B
Missing proofs in Section
4

The formal definitions for the operations
\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{nextM}(\mathbf{a}_{W})
and
\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{prevM}(\mathbf{a}_{W})
are defined in Algorithm
5
and
6
, respectively.

Input:

a list node
\mathsf{currNode}
from
\mathcal{L}_{v}
, a tuple
\mathbf{a}_{W}
with
W\supseteq U\setminus\{v\}

Global variables of
\mathcal{L}_{v}
:

U\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
,
\{v\}=N_{0}\subseteq N_{1},N_{2}\dots,N_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{-}
s.t.
v\in U
,
U\subseteq N_{1}\subseteq N_{2}\subseteq\dots\subseteq N_{i}

Output:

the next node of
\mathsf{currNode}
following
\mathbf{a}_{W}

\ell\leftarrow
the largest in
\{0,1,\dots,m\}
such that
\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{next}[\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{W}]
is defined

return

\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{next}[\Pi_{N_{\ell}\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{W}]

Algorithm 5

\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{nextM}(\mathbf{a}_{W})

Input:

a list node
\mathsf{currNode}
from
\mathcal{L}_{v}
, a tuple
\mathbf{a}_{W}
with
W\supseteq U\setminus\{v\}

Global variables of
\mathcal{L}_{v}
:

U\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
,
\{v\}=N_{0}\subseteq N_{1},N_{2}\dots,N_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{-}
s.t.
v\in U
,
U\subseteq N_{1}\subseteq N_{2}\subseteq\dots\subseteq N_{i}

Output:

the previous node of
\mathsf{currNode}
following
\mathbf{a}_{W}

\ell\leftarrow
the largest in
\{0,1,\dots,m\}
such that
\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{prev}[\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{W}]
is defined

return

\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{prev}[\Pi_{N_{\ell}\setminus\{v\}}\mathbf{a}_{W}]

Algorithm 6

\mathsf{currNode}.\mathsf{prevM}(\mathbf{a}_{W})

Example B.1.

Consider the query
Q(=Q_{4})
in Example
3.6
, the database
\mathcal{D}
in Figure
4
(a) and a
signed
-elimination sequence
\sigma=(1,2,3,4)
.

\displaystyle Q_{4}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})

\displaystyle\leftarrow A(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\land U(x_{3},x_{4})\land\lnot V(x_{4})\land\lnot R(x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\land\lnot S(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})

Preprocessing step.

We demonstrate the recursive steps of Algorithm
1
on inputs
(\mathcal{H}_{4},\mathcal{D}_{4},(1,2,3,4))
.

Step
(1). We remove the signed leaf
x_{4}
from
\mathcal{H}_{4}
, which yields the following query
Q_{3}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})

The database is changed to
\mathcal{D}_{1}

(d).

Step
(4). We remove
x_{1}
from
\mathcal{H}_{1}
, and this step essentially creates a linked-list
\mathcal{L}_{1}
(shown in Figure
4
(h)) on the remaining elements in the relation
A
in Figure

Enumeration step.

For the enumeration step, we first enumerate every element in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
(yielding
\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{2}
and
\mathsf{a}_{3}
).
Then we use that element enumerated in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
as a probing tuple in the enumeration process of every element in
\mathcal{L}_{2}
. For example,
\mathcal{L}_{2}.\mathsf{nextM}(\mathsf{a}_{1})
leads to
\mathsf{b}_{1}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{2}.\mathsf{nextM}(\mathsf{a}_{2})
leads to
\mathsf{b}_{2}
.
We continue this step using the combined tuple enumerated from
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
(say,
(\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{1})
), to enumerate the elements in
\mathcal{L}_{3}
. For example,
\mathcal{L}_{3}.\mathsf{nextM}((\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{1}))
gives
c_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{3}.\mathsf{nextM}((\mathsf{a}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{2}))
gives
c_{2}
.

Finally, we use the combined tuple enumerated from
\mathcal{L}_{1}
,
\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
\mathcal{L}_{3}
(say,
(\mathsf{a}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{2})
), to enumerate the elements in
\mathcal{L}_{4}
.
This step uses the skipping links: for example,
\mathcal{L}_{4}.\mathsf{nextM}((\mathsf{a}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{2}))
would locate the doubly linked-list stored at the key
\mathsf{c}_{2}
, and then we traverse that doubly linked-list using the tuple
(\mathsf{a}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{2})
.
We first yield
\mathsf{d}_{1}
, but since the skipping links leaving
\mathsf{d}_{1}
contain
(\mathsf{a}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{2})
, we follow that skipping-link and reach
\mathsf{d}_{5}
, bypassing
\mathsf{d}_{4}
and correctly enumerating
(\mathsf{a}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{2},\mathsf{d}_{1})
and
(\mathsf{a}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{2},\mathsf{d}_{5})
as answers.

.

\mathsf{BuildList}(v,U)

Lemma
B.2
establishes the correctness of our preprocessing/enumeration algorithms.

Lemma B.2.

Let
Q
be a
signed
-acyclic full
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
and
\mathcal{D}
be a database instance.
Let
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot v
be a
signed
-elimination sequence of the signed hypergraph of
Q
.
Let
Q^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
,
\sigma^{\prime}
be the input to the recursive call of Algorithm
1
.
Then the following statements hold:

(1)

if
\mathbf{a}_{\sigma}\in Q(\mathcal{D})
, then
\mathbf{a}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\in Q^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
; and

(2)

if
