is the
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
expression associated with
\mathcal{H}_{n-1}=\langle{\mathcal{H}},{n}\rangle
obtained by directly peeling off in
x_{n}
from
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
, thus it is recognized by
\textsf{CFG}_{n-1}
(
10
) and
|\psi_{n-1}|=O(|\psi_{n}|)
,

(2)

\mathcal{D}_{n-1}
is a database instance of
\psi_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{[n-1]})
such that
\bigoplus_{x_{n}\in\mathsf{Dom}(x_{n})}\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})=\psi_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{[n-1]})
and
|\mathcal{D}_{n-1}|=O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
.

and the algorithm runs in
O(|\psi_{n}|+d(n)\cdot|\mathcal{D}_{n}|\cdot\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
time, where
d(n)
is the number of hyperedges containing
n
in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

The inverse Ackermann factor
\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
in the aggregation algorithm is inherited from the algorithm supporting
RangeSum
queries. Thus, the guarantee of the
RangeSum
problem carries over to our aggregation algorithm: for semigroups that accepts additive inverse, or for the semigroup with minimum (or maximum) as the operation (
RMQ
as in
(
Gabow et al. 1984
)
), the aggregation algorithm runs in
O(|\psi_{n}|\cdot|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
time, without the additional inverse Ackermann overhead.

C.5.
The oracle-construction algorithm

RangeSum
Chazelle and Rosenberg
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1989
)
studied the range query problem
RangeSum
in the semigroup model defined as follows. Preprocess a array
A
of
w
elements from a semigroup
\mathbf{S}=(\boldsymbol{D},\oplus)
, and then support the following query: given a query range
[\omega^{-},\omega^{+}]
, return the range sum
\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega^{-},\omega^{+}]}A[\omega]
, i.e.
\bigoplus_{\omega^{-}\leq\omega\leq\omega^{+}}A[\omega]
. In particular, they proved the following theorem.

Theorem C.8 (
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1989
)
).

There is a data structure of size
O(w)
that works in the word RAM, and support a
RangeSum
query in
O(\alpha(14w,w))
time, where
\alpha
is the inverse Ackermann function, after spending
O(w)
preprocessing time.

It is the absence of additive inverse for general semirings that results in the hardness of
\mathsf{RangeSum}
and an unconditional hard instance can be recursively constructed that mimics the definition of the inverse Ackermann function
\alpha(\cdot,\cdot)
as in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1989
)
. It is easy to see that
RangeSum
queries can be answered in
O(1)
time after
O(w)
preprocessing, if the underlying semigroup
\mathbf{S}
allows for additive inverse: one candidate algorithm can precompute the partial sums
\bigoplus_{\omega\in[1,\omega^{+}]}A[\omega]
and represent each
RangeSum
query
[\omega^{-},\omega^{+}]
as the semigroup sum of
\bigoplus_{\omega\in[1,\omega^{+}]}A[\omega]
and the additive inverse of
\bigoplus_{\omega\in[1,\omega^{-}-1]}A[\omega]
.

The
RangeSum
problem with minimum as the semigroup operation, is studied intensively and typically known as
RangeMinimumQuery
(
RMQ
). In their influential paper
(
Gabow et al. 1984
)
, Gabow, Bentley and Tarjan shown that there is an algorithm that works in the word RAM and supports a
RMQ
query in
O(1)
time, after spending
O(w)
preprocessing time and space. They recognized the Cartesian tree as the instrumental data structure that was introduced by Vuillemin
(
Vuillemin 1980
)
in the context of average time analysis of searching.

In this section, we introduce a key oracle (called
RangeSumOrcale
) that supports efficient computation of the new negative factors
R_{N\setminus\{n\}}
in the aggregation algorithm introduced in (
17
) of the last section. In particular,
RangeSumOrcale
uses the
RangeSum
data structures as a black-box, thus inherits the inverse Ackermann factor of Theorem
C.8
under general semirings. We first introduce the following definition.

RangeSumOracle

Let
A
be an an array of size
w
over a semigroup
\mathbf{S}=(\boldsymbol{D},\oplus)
, where we implicitly assume that
A[\omega]=\mathbf{0}
if
\omega\notin[1,w]
. An
array decomposition
of
A
is an array of pairs as follows:

\displaystyle\left([1,\omega_{1}],\bigoplus_{\omega\in[1,\omega_{1}]}A[\omega]\right),\;\left([\omega_{1}+1,\omega_{2}],\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega_{1}+1,\omega_{2}]}A[\omega]\right),\;\ldots,\left([\omega_{r}+1,w],\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega_{p}+1,w]}A[\omega]\right),\;\left([w+1,\bot],\mathbf{0}\right)

where
1\leq\omega_{1}\leq\omega_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\omega_{p}=w
(
\omega_{i}
, where
i\in[p]
, and
0,\bot
, are called the
break points
of the array decomposition) and
each pair contains a range and a semigroup sum over that range. For convenience, we add a dummy pair
([w+1,\bot],\mathbf{0})
indicating the end of the array decomposition. A
trivial
array decomposition of
A
is the array of pairs

\displaystyle\left([1,1],A[1]\right),\;\left([2,2],A[2]\right),\;\ldots,\left([w,w],A[w]\right),\;\left([w+1,\bot],\mathbf{0}\right).

whose set of break points is
\{1,2,\ldots,w\}
.

An
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}
of the array
A
is a
\mathsf{RangeSum}
data structure built on an array decomposition of
A
such that: given a query range
[\omega_{i}+1,\omega_{j}]
, where
\omega_{i}<\omega_{j}
are two break points of the array decomposition (so that the query range aligns with the smaller range sums stored in the array decomposition), it returns the range sum over the query range

\bigoplus_{i\leq k<j}\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega_{k}+1,\omega_{k+1}]}A[\omega]=\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega_{i}+1,\omega_{j}]}A[\omega]

It is easy to see from
Theorem C.8
that there is a data structure of size
O(w)
and support a
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}
query in
O(\alpha(14w,w))
time, where
\alpha(\cdot,\cdot)
is the inverse Ackermann function, after spending
O(w)
preprocessing time.
Indeed, for a trivial array decomposition, the
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}
is essentially a
\mathsf{RangeSum}
data structure of
A
.

Now we formally describe our
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}
(s). To better identify
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
subexpressions show up in
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
, we let
\ell_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\Rightarrow}}\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}})
for
n\in U=N_{0}\subseteq N^{-}_{1}\subseteq N_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq N^{-}_{k}\subseteq N_{k}
and
\ell_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\Rightarrow}}\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S})
for all
n\notin S\subseteq[n]
. Then, (
16
) can be equivalently written as:

(18)

\displaystyle\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})

\displaystyle=\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{k}})\otimes\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k}})

\displaystyle=\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{k}})\otimes\left(R_{N_{k}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k}})\vdash\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N^{-}_{k}})\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{k}})\otimes\left(R_{N_{k}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k}})\vdash\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{k-1}})\otimes\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k-1}})\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{k}})\otimes\left(R_{N_{k}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k}})\vdash\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{k-1}})\otimes\left(R_{N_{k-1}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k-1}})\vdash\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N^{-}_{k-1}})\right)\right)

\displaystyle\vdots

\displaystyle=\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{k}})\otimes\left(R_{N_{k}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k}})\vdash\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{k-1}})\otimes\left(R_{N_{k-1}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k-1}})\vdash\cdots R_{N_{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{1}})\vdash\left(\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{1}})\otimes(R_{N_{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{1}})\vdash\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N^{-}_{1}}))\right)\cdots\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{k}})\otimes\left(R_{N_{k}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k}})\vdash\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{k-1}})\otimes\left(R_{N_{k-1}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k-1}})\vdash\cdots R_{N_{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{1}})\vdash\left(\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{1}})\otimes(R_{N_{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{1}})\vdash\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{0}})\otimes\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{U}))\right)\cdots\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{k}})\otimes\left(R_{N_{k}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k}})\vdash\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{k-1}})\otimes\left(R_{N_{k-1}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k-1}})\vdash\cdots R_{N_{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{1}})\vdash\left(\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{1}})\otimes(R_{N_{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{1}})\vdash\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{0}})\otimes R_{U}(\mathbf{x}_{U}))\right)\cdots\right)\right)

We will construct a
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}
for the
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
subexpression
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{U})
(let
U=N_{0}
) and then
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{1}}),\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{2}}),\ldots,\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{k}})
, in a bottom-up order as layed out in (
18
). Indeed, the
\mathsf{BuildOracle}
algorithm described in Algorithm
9
takes as input the
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
expression
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
recognized by
\textsf{CFG}_{n}
and the corresponding database instance
\mathcal{D}_{n}
and returns a
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}
for the every
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
subexpression
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}})
, where
i=0,1,\ldots,k
. Now we walk through its steps, starting from the inner-most
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{U})=R_{U}(\mathbf{x}_{U})
in (
18
),
R_{U}
being the pivot factor.

Input:

a
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
expression
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
recognized by
\textsf{CFG}_{n}
and a global database instance
\mathcal{D}_{n}

Output:

An
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}

\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
for each
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}})
, where
i=0,1,\ldots,k
and
U=N_{0}\subseteq N_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq N_{k}
are the only hyperedges containing
n

1

foreach

\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k}\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{i}}

do

\triangleright
base case for
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{U})

2

init

A_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})\leftarrow[]
,
\mathcal{T}_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})\leftarrow[]
,
\omega_{\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}}\leftarrow 1

3

foreach

\mathbf{a}_{U}\in\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k}\Pi_{U}R_{N_{i}}

do

4

A_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})[\omega_{\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}}]\leftarrow\Pi_{\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{U}

append

\left([\omega_{\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}},\omega_{\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}}],R_{U}(\mathbf{a}_{U})\right)

to

\mathcal{T}_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})

\omega_{\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}}\leftarrow\omega_{\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}}+1

foreach

\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k}\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{i}}

5

append

\left([\omega_{\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}}+1,\bot],\;\mathbf{0}\right)

\mathcal{T}_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})

construct

\mathsf{RangeSum}
data structures
on

\mathcal{T}_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})

6

foreach

i=1,2,\ldots k
(in order)

do

\triangleright
inductive case for
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}})

7

foreach

\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{s}}

8

\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\leftarrow[]
,
\omega^{-}_{\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}}\leftarrow 0

9

\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{i-1\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{s}}

M_{1}\leftarrow\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega^{-}_{\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}}+1,\omega^{+}-1]}\psi_{n}\left(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\right)

\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

to the next break point of
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})

\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{s}}

do

13

([\omega^{-}_{\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}}+1,\bot],\;\mathbf{0})
into
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

construct

\mathsf{RangeSum}
data structures
on

\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

return

\left(\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\right)_{i=0,1,\ldots,k}

Algorithm 9

\mathsf{BuildOracle}(\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]}),\mathcal{D}_{n})

the base case of
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{U})=R_{U}(\mathbf{x}_{U})
.

The base case first sets up the indices for accessing all
x_{n}
values for
\mathsf{RangeSum}
data structures constructed later. More precisely, we first build a hashtable
A_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
from
\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k}\Pi_{U}R_{N_{i}}
, where
\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}}
is its key and each entry
\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}}=\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}\in\mathcal{T}_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
stores an array of
x_{n}
values where
(x_{n},\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})\in\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k}\Pi_{U}R_{N_{i}}
. The array is identified as
A_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
and the ordering of
x_{n}
values in the array can be posited arbitrarily but fixed afterwards. Thus,
A_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
is of size
|\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k}\Pi_{U}R_{N_{i}}|=O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
and allows for every tuple
\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k}\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{i}}
, a direct (array) access to all possible
x_{n}
values that could appear alongside
\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}
in the database instance
\mathcal{D}_{n}
. It is easy to see that this construction costs
O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
time and space.

Now we start constructing the base-case oracle
\mathcal{T}_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
for
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{U})=R_{U}(\mathbf{x}_{U})
. It follows exactly as
A_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
except that each entry
\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}}=\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}\in\mathcal{T}_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
stores instead a trivial array decomposition of
A_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
, replacing each value of
x_{n}
in
A_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
by its weight
R_{U}(x_{n},\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})\in\boldsymbol{D}
. So the
\omega
-th element in
A_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
, say
a_{n}=A_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})[\omega]
, corresponds to
the
\omega
-th entry in the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
, i.e.
\left([\omega,\omega],R_{U}(a_{n},\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})\right)=\left([\omega,\omega],\psi_{n}(a_{n},\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})\right)
. Thus,
\mathcal{T}_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
is of size
|A_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}})|=O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
. We recall that for
(a_{n},\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})\notin R_{U}
, we have
R_{U}(a_{n},\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})=\mathbf{0}
. Applying
Theorem C.8
, we construct a
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}
on each array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
, after spending
O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
preprocessing time building the
\mathsf{RangeSum}
data structures. Abusing notations, we denote the entire oracle as

\mathcal{T}_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
to implicitly indicate that it is a hashtable of array decompositions equipped with the
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}
that supports the following query in
O(\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
time:

oracle:

\mathcal{T}_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}})

input:

a tuple
\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k}\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{i}}
(to identity the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
to be queried) and a query range
[\omega^{-}+1,\omega^{+}]
, where
0\leq\omega^{-}<\omega^{+}
are two break points of the array decomposition.

output:

a range sum
\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega^{-}+1,\omega^{+}]}\psi_{n}\left(A_{0}(\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\;\mathbf{a}_{U\setminus\{n\}}\right)
.

the inductive case of
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}})
.

For
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}})
, where
i\in[k]
, following (
18
), we have that
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}})=R_{N_{i}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}})\vdash\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N^{-}_{i}})=R_{N_{i}}(x_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\vdash\mu_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{i-1}})\otimes\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}})
. Thus, for a fixing of
\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}=\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}
, we have

(19)

\displaystyle\psi_{n}(x_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

\displaystyle=R_{N_{i}}(x_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\vdash\psi_{n}(x_{n},\Pi_{N^{-}_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

\displaystyle=R_{N_{i}}(x_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\vdash\mu_{n}(\Pi_{S_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\otimes\psi_{n}(x_{n},\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

Following the bottom-up construction, for the
i
-th
(i>0)
time entering in the for-loop on
algorithm 9
, we have constructed the
(i-1)
-th
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}
(s) denoted as
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
for
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}})
. Specifically, the
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}
(s)
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
is a hashtable of size
O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
containing array decompositions as entries that supports the following range query in
O(\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
time:

oracle:

\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})

input:

a tuple
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{i-1\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{s}}
(i.e. a query key to identity the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
to be queried) and a query range
[\omega^{-}+1,\omega^{+}]
, where
0\leq\omega^{-}\leq\omega^{+}
are two break points of the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
. In particular, the break points of the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
are
\omega
and
\omega-1
, where

\displaystyle\omega\in\left\{\omega\mid\left(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\right)\in\bigcup_{i-1\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i-1}}R_{N_{s}}\right\}

output:

a range sum
\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega^{-}+1,\omega^{+}]}\psi_{n}\left(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\;\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\right)
, (or
\mathbf{0}
if
\omega^{-}=\omega^{+}
).

We now start constructing a new
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}

\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
for
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}})
, using the
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}

\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
. We initialize
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
as a hashtable with
\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}
as its key, where each entry
\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}=\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{t}\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{s}}
is intialized as an empty array decomposition, denoted as
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
. Furthermore, we initialize a set of scanning iterators
\omega^{-}_{\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}}=0
, one for each
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{t}\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{s}}
that pinpoints the last constructed break point throughout its ongoing construction process.

Next, we will make one scan over every entry (array decomposition) stored in
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
by iterating over its break points, and for each array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
, we construct the array decompositions
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
for all
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{t}\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{s}}
such that
\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}=\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}
simultaneously. Let us now describe the exact steps when scanning an array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
in the oracle
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
. First, we initialize a scanning iterator
\omega^{+}=1
to scan the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
by moving
\omega^{+}
forward to the next break point.

As we are scanning at the break point
\omega^{+}
of the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
, we retrieve its corresponding
x_{n}
value via
A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega^{+}]=a_{n}
. For every ongoing construction of
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
(i.e.
\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}=\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}
), if
(a_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i}}R_{N_{s}}
, we set both
\omega^{+}-1
and
\omega^{+}
as it new break points by appending the following two pairs to the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
:

(1)

the first pair to be appended is

\displaystyle\left([\omega^{-}_{\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}}+1,\omega^{+}-1],\;\mu_{n}(\Pi_{S_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\otimes M_{1}\right)

where
M_{1}
is a call to the
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}

\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
with the input parameters
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}=\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}
(the query key) and
[\omega^{-}_{\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}}+1,\omega^{+}-1]
(the query range). Therefore, by induction hypothesis,

\displaystyle M_{1}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega^{-}_{\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}}+1,\omega^{+}-1]}\psi_{n}\left(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\right)

and
M_{1}
is returned by
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
in time
O(\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
.

(2)

the second pair to be appended is

\displaystyle\left([\omega^{+},\omega^{+}],\;R_{N_{i}}(a_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\oplus\mathbbm{1}_{{\neg R_{N_{i}}}}(a_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\otimes\mu_{n}(\Pi_{S_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\otimes M_{2}\right)

where
M_{2}
is a call to the
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}

\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
with the input parameters
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}=\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}
(the query key) and
[\omega^{+},\omega^{+}]
(the query range). Therefore, by induction hypothesis,

\displaystyle M_{2}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega^{+},\omega^{+}]}\psi_{n}\left(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\right)=\psi_{n}(a_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})=\psi_{n}(a_{n},\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

and
M_{2}
is returned by
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
in time
O(\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
.

The correctness of the second pair simply follows from (
19
), where we fix the value of
x_{n}
to be
a_{n}=A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega]
. We now reason the correctness of the first pair as follows: by construction of break points,
\omega^{-}_{\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}}
and
\omega^{+}
are two consecutive break points for
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
, thus

(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\notin R_{N_{i}}
, for all
\omega\in[\omega^{-}_{\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}}+1,\omega^{+}-1]
. Therefore,
R_{N_{i}}(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega])=\mathbf{0}
and

\mathbbm{1}_{{R_{N_{i}}}}(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega])=\mathbf{1}
for all
\omega\in[\omega^{-}_{\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}}+1,\omega^{+}-1]
. Still following (
19
), we have that

\displaystyle\psi_{n}(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

\displaystyle=R_{N_{i}}(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega])\oplus\mathbbm{1}_{{R_{N_{i}}}}(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

\displaystyle\otimes\psi_{n}(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\Pi_{N^{-}_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0}\oplus\mathbf{1}\otimes\psi_{n}(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\;\Pi_{N^{-}_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

\displaystyle=\psi_{n}(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\;\Pi_{N^{-}_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

\displaystyle=\mu_{n}(\Pi_{S_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\otimes\psi_{n}(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\;\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

Hence, the correctness of the first pair follows because

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega^{-}_{\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}}+1,\omega^{+}-1]}\psi_{n}(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})=\mu_{n}(\Pi_{S_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\otimes M_{1}.

As a summary, one scan over the break points of every array decomposition stored in
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
costs time
O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
, i.e. the size of the oracle. Furthermore, for a fixed
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{i-1\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{s}}
, we make break points across all the (constructing) array decompositions of
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
where
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i}}R_{N_{s}}
and
\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}=\mathbf{a}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}
and by construction, the break points are
\omega
and
\omega-1
where

\displaystyle\omega\in\left\{\omega\mid\left(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\right)\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i}}R_{N_{s}}\right\}

Observe that the break points constructed in
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
is a subset of those of
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
, thus we are free from the risk of asking the oracle
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\Pi_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
with mis-aligned intervals, when constructing
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
. The number of break points to be constructed in the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
is at most
\sum_{i\leq s\leq k}\mathsf{deg}_{R_{N_{s}}}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
, where
\mathsf{deg}_{R_{N_{s}}}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
denotes the degree of
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}
in
R_{N_{s}}
, i.e., the number of tuples in
R_{N_{s}}
that coincide with
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}
on the variables
\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}
. Therefore, the total number of break points in the oracle
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
is at most

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{s}}}\sum_{i\leq s\leq k}\mathsf{deg}_{R_{N_{s}}}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})\leq\sum_{i\leq s\leq k}|R_{N_{s}}|=O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|).

The new-built oracle
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
is thus of size
O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
. Furthermore, for each break point, it costs
O(\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
to get the two range sums
M_{1},M_{2}
from the oracle
\mathcal{T}_{i-1}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i-1}\setminus\{n\}})
, thus the total time of the construction of
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
is
O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|\cdot\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
. The
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}

\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
supports the following range query in time
O(\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
:

oracle:

\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

input:

a tuple
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{s}}
(i.e. a query key to identity the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
to be queried) and a query range
[\omega^{-}+1,\omega^{+}]
, where
0\leq\omega^{-}\leq\omega^{+}
are two break points of the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
. In particular, the break points of the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
are
\omega
and
\omega-1
, where

\displaystyle\omega\in\left\{\omega\mid\left(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\right)\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i}}R_{N_{s}}\right\}

output:

a range sum
\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega^{-}+1,\omega^{+}]}\psi_{n}\left(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\;\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\right)
, (or
\mathbf{0}
if
\omega^{-}=\omega^{+}
).

To conclude this section, we present the following theorem, whose proof is immediate from the prior discussion in this section. Here,
k=d(n)
, where we recall that
d(n)
is number of hyperedges containing the
signed
-leaf
n
in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

Theorem C.9.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{n}
be a signed hypergraph with a
signed
-leaf
n
. Let
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
be a
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
expression (
16
) associated with
\mathcal{H}_{n}
(thus recognized by
\textsf{CFG}_{n}
(
9
)) and
\mathcal{D}_{n}
be its database instance. Suppose that
U=N_{0}\subseteq N_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq N_{k}
are the only hyperedges containing the
signed
-leaf
n
in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Then, there is an algorithm that takes
(\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]}),\mathcal{D}_{n})
as input and constructs in
O(|\psi_{n}|+k\cdot|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
time, a
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}

\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
, for every
i=0,1,\ldots,k
, and the oracle
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
is of size
O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
and supports the following range query in
O(\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
time:

oracle:

\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

input:

a query key
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{s}}
to identity the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
to be queried, and a query range
[\omega^{-}+1,\omega^{+}]
, where
0\leq\omega^{-}\leq\omega^{+}
are two break points of the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
. In particular, the break points of the array decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
are
\omega
and
\omega-1
, where

\displaystyle\omega\in\left\{\omega\mid\left(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\right)\in\bigcup_{i\leq s\leq k}\Pi_{N_{i}}R_{N_{s}}\right\}

output:

a range sum
\bigoplus_{\omega\in[\omega^{-}+1,\omega^{+}]}\psi_{n}\left(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\;\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\right)
, (or
\mathbf{0}
if
\omega^{-}=\omega^{+}
).

C.6.
The
signed
-elimination step: a summary

In this section, we put together the discussions in Appendix
C.3
, Appendix
C.5
and Appendix
C.4
to prove the main theorem for the
signed
-elimination sequence, i.e.
Theorem C.2
. First, we give a proof for
Theorem C.7
, now equipped with the
\mathsf{RangeSumOracle}
described in
subsection C.5
.

Proof of Theorem
C.7
.

The aggregation algorithm follows (
17
) line-by-line and “pushes” the aggregation operator
\bigoplus_{x_{n}}
into the inner-most parenthesis, therefore costing time
O(|\psi_{n}|)
to scan over the derivation of
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
. Factoring out the term
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{S_{i}})
, where
i=0,\ldots,k=d(n)
and
n\notin S_{i}
is a pure syntactic step. The last step aggregates out
x_{n}
from the pivot factor and gets a new positive factor
R_{U\setminus\{n\}}(\mathbf{x}_{U\setminus\{n\}})
in
O(|R_{U}|)
time and space.

The only tricky step in (
17
) for pushing the aggregation operator
\bigoplus_{x_{n}}
into the inner-most parenthesis is to efficiently compute the list representation of
R_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
, where
i=1,\ldots,k=d(n)
, and by definition,

\displaystyle R_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})=\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{x_{n}}\psi_{n}(x_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})&\text{ if }\mathbf{a}_{N\setminus\{n\}}\in\Pi_{N\setminus\{n\}}R_{N}\\
\mathbf{0}&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

where
\psi_{n}(x_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})=R_{N_{i}}({x_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N\setminus\{n\}}})\oplus\mathbbm{1}_{{\neg R_{N_{i}}}}({x_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}})\otimes\psi_{n}({x_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N^{-}_{i}\setminus\{n\}}})
. Now we apply
Theorem C.9
to get the oracles
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
for each
i=1,\ldots,k
. Then, the weight
R_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
can be obtained in
O(\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
time, for each
\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\in\Pi_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}R_{N_{i}}
, by calling the oracle
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})
with inputs
\left(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}},[1,\bot]\right)
. Indeed, the oracle runs in time
O(\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
and returns the range sum

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\omega\in[1,\bot]}\psi_{n}\left(A_{0}(\Pi_{U\setminus\{n\}}\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})[\omega],\;\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}\right)=\bigoplus_{x_{n}\in\mathsf{Dom}(x_{n})}\psi_{n}(x_{n},\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})=R_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}})

Therefore, the list representation of the new factor
R_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}
can be computed in
O(|R_{N_{i}}|\cdot\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
time and is of size
O(|R_{N_{i}}|)
. The overall time complexity of the aggregation algorithm is
O(d(n)\cdot|\mathcal{D}_{n}|\cdot\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
since there are
d(n)
steps in the derivation of
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
that requires the construction of a new factor
R_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}
and each construction takes at most
O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|\cdot\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
time (to further push the aggregation operator
\bigoplus_{x_{n}}
into the nested subexpression). After we aggregate out the variable
x_{n}
from
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
, we automatically get a new
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
expression
\psi_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{[n-1]})
. Since we keep the derivation of
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
intact, we have
|\psi_{n-1}|=O(|\psi_{n}|)
and
\psi_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{[n-1]})
is indeed a
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
expression recognized by
\textsf{CFG}_{n-1}
. Moreover, as all the new factors
R_{N_{i}\setminus\{n\}}
are still of size
O(|R_{N_{i}}|)
, we have
|\mathcal{D}_{n-1}|=O(|\mathcal{D}_{n}|)
.
∎

We finally prove the main theorem
Theorem C.2
for free-connex
signed
-acyclic
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
queries.

Proof of
Theorem C.2
.

Recall that for a
signed
-leaf, say
n
, we run the following algorithms in order:

(1)

first, by
Theorem C.6
, we apply the
\mathsf{Refactor}
algorithm in time
O(|\psi|^{2}+d(n)\cdot|\mathcal{D}|)
, where
d(n)
is the number of hyperedges in
\mathcal{H}
that contain
n
, to transform
(\psi(\mathbf{x}_{[n]}),\mathcal{D})
into a
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
expression
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
recognized by
\textsf{CFG}_{n}
(
9
) and a database instance
\mathcal{D}_{n}
such that
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})=\psi(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
. Moreover, we have that
|\psi_{n}|\leq|\psi|
and
|\mathcal{D}_{n}|\leq|\mathcal{D}|
.

(2)

next, we run the oracle-construction algorithm described in Appendix
C.5
for
\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
. By
Theorem C.9
, the construction of oracles takes
O(|\psi_{n}|+|\mathcal{D}_{n}|\cdot\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
time.

(3)

finally, we apply the aggregation algorithm on
(\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]}),\mathcal{D}_{n})
and by
Theorem C.7
, we obtain in
O(|\psi_{n}|+d(n)\cdot|\mathcal{D}_{n}|\cdot\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}_{n}|,|\mathcal{D}_{n}|))
time the tuple
(\psi_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{[n-1]}),\mathcal{D}_{n-1})
, where
\psi_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{[n-1]})
is a
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
expression associated with
\mathcal{H}_{n-1}=\langle{\mathcal{H}_{n}},{n-1}\rangle
and
\mathcal{D}_{n-1}
is its database instance such that
\psi_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{[n-1]})=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{x}_{[n]\setminus F}\in\mathsf{Dom}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]\setminus F})}\;\psi_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})
. By
Theorem C.7
, we have that
|\psi_{n-1}|\leq|\psi_{n}|
and
|\mathcal{D}_{n-1}|\leq|\mathcal{D}_{n}|
. Thereafter, recall that
\psi_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{n-1})
is recognized by the following
\textsf{CFG}_{n-1}
(
10
)

(20)

\displaystyle\framebox{$\textsf{CFG}_{n-1}$}

\displaystyle\text{ for all }S\subseteq[n-1],

\displaystyle\ell_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{\emptyset})

\displaystyle::=\;e

\displaystyle\text{ where }e\in\boldsymbol{D}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}

\displaystyle\ell_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{S})

\displaystyle::=\;R_{K}(\mathbf{x}_{K})\otimes\ell_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{S^{-}}),

\displaystyle\text{ where }K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}_{n-1},S^{-}\subseteq S\text{ such that }S=K\cup S^{-}

\displaystyle\mid\;(R_{N}(\mathbf{x}_{N})\vdash\ell_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{N^{-}}))\otimes\ell_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{S^{-}}),

\displaystyle\text{ where }N\in\mathcal{E}^{-}_{n-1},N^{-}\subseteq N,S^{-}\subseteq S\text{ such that }S=N\cup S^{-}

Thus, the
signed
-elimination step on
n
takes
O(|\psi|^{2}+d(n)\cdot|\mathcal{D}|\cdot\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}|,|\mathcal{D}|))
time in total.

Now we are ready for the next
signed
-elimination step for
n-2
with
signed
-leaf
n-1
. We keep repeating the
signed
-elimination step for
i=n-2,\dots,f
and every intermediate
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
expression
\psi_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{[i]})
and its corresponding database instance
\mathcal{D}_{i}
have their sizes bounded by
O(|\psi|)
and
O(|\mathcal{D}|)
, respectively. In the end, we obtain a full
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
expression
\psi_{f}(\mathbf{x}_{[f]})
and a database instance
\mathcal{D}_{f}
as desired, i.e.

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{F})=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{x}_{[n]\setminus F}\in\mathsf{Dom}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]\setminus F})}\;\psi(\mathbf{x}_{[n]})=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{x}_{[n-1]\setminus F}\in\mathsf{Dom}(\mathbf{x}_{[n-1]\setminus F})}\;\psi_{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{[n-1]})=\cdots=\psi_{f}(\mathbf{x}_{[f]}).

Therefore, the total time for the
signed
-elimination sequence (for
i=n-1,\dots,f
) is bounded by (by definition,
|\varphi|=|\psi|
)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=f}^{n-1}O(|\psi|^{2}+d(i)\cdot|\mathcal{D}|\cdot\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}|,|\mathcal{D}|))=O(|\psi|^{3}+|\psi|\cdot|\mathcal{D}|\cdot\alpha(14|\mathcal{D}|,|\mathcal{D}|)).

From now on, we follow the enumeration algorithm for full
\mathsf{NestFAQ}^{\neg}
queries proposed in Appendix
C.1
and by Appendix
C.4
, thus we close the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Missing proofs in Section
6

D.1.
Some Notations and Results

We apply a characterization for
\alpha
-acyclic hypergraphs by Beeri, Fagin, Maier and Yannakakis
(
Beeri et al. 1983
)
. The
Gaifman graph
of a hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
is the clique graph of
\mathcal{H}
, i.e., we replace every hyperedge of size
k
with a clique of size
k
over the variables in the hyperedge.

Definition D.1 (Conformal Hypergraph).

A hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
is called
conformal
if for every clique in its Gaifman graph, there exists an atom covering the clique.

Definition D.2 (Chordal Hypergraph).

A hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
is called
chordal
if every cycle of length at least 4 in its Gaifman graph has a chord.

Theorem D.3 (Beeri et al., 83’).

A hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
is
\alpha
-acyclic if and only if it is conformal and chordal.

We also reprove a lemma which strengthens Theorem
D.3
for signed hypergraphs
(
Brault-Baron 2013
)
. The result originally appeared in French
(
Brault-Baron 2013
)
.
Given a signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
, for some
S\subseteq[n]
we denote by
\mathcal{H}[S]
the induced signed hypergraph on the vertex set
S
.

Lemma D.4 (Brault-Baron, 13’).

If a signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
is not
\alpha
-acyclic, then we can find a set of vertices
S\subseteq[n]
, with
|S|\geq 3
, such that:

-

the Gaifman graph of the positive hyperedges in
\mathcal{H}[S]
is a clique of size
|S|
;

-

there exists a subset
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
of the negative hyperedges in
\mathcal{H}[S]
such that the Gaifman graph of the union of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
with the positive atoms in
\mathcal{H}[S]
is a
|S|
-cycle.

Proof.

Take a minimal set of vertices
S
such that
\mathcal{H}[S]
is not
\alpha
-acyclic. Take the minimal set
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}
such that the hypergraph with edges
\mathcal{E}^{+}[S]\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is not
\alpha
-acyclic. By Theorem
D.3
, there exists a set of vertices
S^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}[S^{\prime}]
is a cycle or a clique. In both cases, the minimality of
S
imposes that
S=S^{\prime}
. If
\mathcal{H}[S^{\prime}]
were a cycle, then we are done. Otherwise,
\mathcal{H}[S^{\prime}]
is a clique. If
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=\emptyset
, then we are also done. Otherwise, let
e\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
. By the minimality of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
, we know that (1)
e\notin\mathcal{E}^{+}
and (2)
e
participates as an edge in the clique
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}[S]
. But then note that
(\mathcal{E}^{+}[S]\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\setminus\{e\})[S\setminus x]
, for the unique
x
where
x\in S
but
x\notin e
, is a clique of smaller size. This contradicts the minimality of
S
. The proof is now complete.
∎

D.2.
Missing proofs in Section
6.1

Proof of Theorem
6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the signed hypergraph of
Q
. We present the proof of Theorem
6.3
when the hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
is simple, i.e. without duplicate hyperedges. The proof can be easily adapted to multi-hypergraphs. We note that our proof does not work for
Q
with self-joins, whose linear time boundary remains to be an open problem.

Since
\mathcal{H}
is not
signed
-acyclic, by Lemma
D.4
, we can split into two cases.
Suppose it is the first case of Lemma
D.4
, then the proof is identical to that in
(
Berkholz et al. 2020
;
Brault-Baron 2013
)
which we omit for brevity, since the clique consists of only positive hyperedges (the instance in the reduction will have every negated atom be empty).

Otherwise, we are in the second case of Lemma
D.4
. Therefore, there are
\ell\geq 3
variables
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{\ell}
that form a chordless cycle (or cycle of length 3) in the Gaifman graph of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\mathcal{H}[S]
. Given any input graph
G
for the
Triangle
problem, we construct our database instance as follows. For every vertex
v
in
G
, we have a fresh domain element
a_{v}
. For any variable
w
not in the set
\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{\ell}\}
, we define its active domain being
\{\perp_{w}\}
, a fresh domain element.
For any
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
, there exists exactly one hyperedge
U_{i}
that contains
x_{i},x_{i+1}
(modulo
\ell
) since the cycle
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{\ell}
is chordless.
For
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, for
U_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
(
U_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{-}
resp.), we define
R_{U_{i}}:=\left\{\left(f_{uv}\left(w_{1}\right),\ldots,f_{uv}\left(w_{r}\right)\right)\mid\{u,v\}\in E(G)\right\}
(
R_{U_{i}}:=\left\{\left(f_{uv}\left(w_{1}\right),\ldots,f_{uv}\left(w_{r}\right)\right)\mid\{u,v\}\notin E(G)\right\}
resp.) where
f_{uv}(x_{i})=a_{u},f_{uv}(x_{i+1})=a_{v}
and
f_{uv}(w)=\perp_{w}
for all other variables
w
in the atom.
For
i\in\{4,\dots,\ell\}
, for
U_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
(
U_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{-}
resp.), we define
R_{U_{i}}:=\left\{\left(g_{uv}\left(w_{1}\right),\ldots,g_{uv}\left(w_{r}\right)\right)\mid u=v\in V(G)\right\}
(
R_{U_{i}}:=\left\{\left(g_{uv}\left(w_{1}\right),\ldots,g_{uv}\left(w_{r}\right)\right)\mid u\neq v\in V(G)\right\}
resp.) where
g_{uv}(x_{i})=a_{u},g_{uv}(x_{i+1})=a_{v}
and
g_{uv}(w)=\perp_{w}
for all other variables
w
in the atom.
For the remaining positive relations, we define
R_{U}:=\left\{\left(h_{v}\left(w_{1}\right),\ldots,h_{v}\left(w_{r}\right)\right)\mid v\in V(G)\right\}
where
h_{v}(x_{j})=a_{v}
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
and
g_{v}(w)=\perp
for all other variables
w
. We set the remaining negative relations to be empty. Note that there could be at most
n^{2}
tuples in each atom, where
n
is the number of vertices in
G
. A moment of reflection should convince the reader that the input graph
G
contains a triangle if and only if the database constructed returns non-empty result for
Q
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.5
.

We adapt the proof from
(
Bagan et al. 2007
;
Berkholz et al. 2020
)
. The main difference is how we handle the negated atoms in the query.

Let
F
be the set of head variables of
Q
. Let
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
be the signed hypergraph of
Q
. Since
\mathcal{H}
is not free-connex, there exists a subset
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}
such that the hypergraph
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=([n],\mathcal{E})
, where
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
, is not free-connex (but it is
\alpha
-acyclic). By
(
Bagan et al. 2007
)
, then
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
admits a sequence of distinct vertices
P=(x,z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{k},y)
with
k\geq 1
, such that

•

x,y\in F
and
z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{k}\notin F
,

•

(
P
is a path) there are
k+1
hyperedges
e_{0},e_{1},\cdots,e_{k-1},e_{k}\in\mathcal{E}
that contain the respective pairs
e_{0}^{\prime}=\left\{x,z_{1}\right\},e_{1}^{\prime}=\left\{z_{1},z_{2}\right\},\ldots,e_{k-1}^{\prime}=\left\{z_{k-1},z_{k}\right\},e_{k}^{\prime}=\left\{z_{k},y\right\}
, and

•

(
P
is chordless) for each
e\in\mathcal{E}
,
|e\cap P|\leq 1
or
|e\cap P|=e_{i}^{\prime}
, for some
0\leq i\leq k
.

W.l.o.g, we assume that in
Q
,
\mathbf{x}_{F}=(x,y,\mathbf{t})
and
\mathbf{x}_{[n]}=(x,y,z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{k},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u})
, so each atom of
Q
is of one of the following types for some
\mathbf{v}\subseteq\{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}\}
:
(1)

R(x,z_{1},\mathbf{v})
;
(2)

R(z_{k},y,\mathbf{v})
;
(3)

R(z_{i},z_{i+1},\mathbf{v})
, where
1\leq i<k
;
(4)

R(w,\mathbf{v})
, where
w\in\{x,z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{k},y\}
on the path
P
, and
(5)

R(\mathbf{v})
.

Now we illustrate the reduction from
\mathsf{BMM}
to the
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}

Q
by constructing an instance
(R_{K})_{K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}}\cup(R_{N})_{N\in\mathcal{E}^{-}}
for
Q
.
Let
A,B
be two binary relations that encode the two given
n\times n
Boolean matrices. Let
I
be the identity relation on the domain
[n]
,
I=\{(a,a)\mid a\in[n]\}
.
The active domain for the variables
\{x,z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{k},y\}
is defined as
[n]
. We introduce
\perp
as a fresh domain element and define the active domain for variables in
\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}
as
\{\perp\}
. Then, for each
K\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
, we construct the relational instance following the proof of Lemma 26 in
(
Bagan et al. 2007
)
. The construction of each negated relation in
\mathcal{E}^{-}
of the corresponding type is simply the complement of its positive counterpart. To be precise, if
K
has arity
p
, we have:

(1)

R(x,z_{1},\mathbf{v})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}A\times\{\perp\}^{p-2},&\text{ if }\{x,z_{1},\mathbf{v}\}\in\mathcal{E}^{+}\\
(\neg A)\times\{\perp\}^{p-2},&\text{ otherwise }(\{x,z_{1},\mathbf{v}\}\in\mathcal{E}^{-})\end{cases}

(2)

R(z_{k},y,\mathbf{v})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}B\times\{\perp\}^{p-2},&\text{ if }\{z_{k},y,\mathbf{v}\}\in\mathcal{E}^{+}\\
(\neg B)\times\{\perp\}^{p-2},&\text{ otherwise }(\{z_{k},y,\mathbf{v}\}\in\mathcal{E}^{-})\end{cases}

(3)

R(z_{i},z_{i+1},\mathbf{v})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}I\times\{\perp\}^{p-2},&\text{ if }\{z_{i},z_{i+1},\mathbf{v}\}\in\mathcal{E}^{+}\\
(\neg I)\times\{\perp\}^{p-2},&\text{ otherwise }(\{z_{i},z_{i+1},\mathbf{v}\}\in\mathcal{E}^{-})\end{cases}

(4)

R(w,\mathbf{v})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}[n]\times\{\perp\}^{p-1},&\text{ if }\{z_{i},z_{i+1},\mathbf{v}\}\in\mathcal{E}^{+}\\
\emptyset,&\text{ otherwise }(\{z_{i},z_{i+1},\mathbf{v}\}\in\mathcal{E}^{-})\end{cases}

(5)

R(\mathbf{v})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\{\perp\}^{p},&\text{ if }\{\mathbf{v}\}\in\mathcal{E}^{+}\\
\emptyset,&\text{ otherwise }(\{\mathbf{v}\}\in\mathcal{E}^{-})\end{cases}

It is easy to see that all relations can be constructed in
O(n^{2})
time and also have size
O(n^{2})
. We prove that the output of
Q
on this relational instance (projecting on
x,y
), denoted as
C
, encodes the resulting matrix of the Boolean matrix multiplication.

Indeed, if
(i,j)
-th entry (in the resulting matrix) is
1
, then there is some
k\in[n]
such that
(i,k)\in A
and
(k,j)\in B
. It is easy to verify that the valuation
\nu(\cdot)
, where
\nu(x)=i,\nu(y)=j,\nu(z_{1})=\cdots=\nu(z_{k})=k
and
\nu(v)=\;\perp
, for
v\in\{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}\}
, is a true valuation (
(i,j)\in C
). If
(i,j)
-th entry (in the resulting matrix) is
0
, for any
k\in[n]
, either
(i,k)\notin A
or
(k,j)\notin B
. That is, there is no valuation that satisfies both
R(x,z_{1},\mathbf{v})
and
R(z_{k},y,\mathbf{v})
. Therefore,
(i,j)\notin C
.

Now that we have proven the reduction to an instance for
Q
of size
O(n^{2})
, a
O(n^{2})
pre-processing time and
O(1)
delay algorithm for
Q
implies that the resulting matrix can be obtained in time
O(n^{2})
, contradicting
\mathsf{BMM}
.
∎

D.3.
Missing proofs in Section
6.2

We begin with the definitions of
k
-clique embedding
and its
weak edge depth
.

Definition D.5 (
k
-Clique Embedding).

Let
C_{k}
be the
k
-clique and
\mathcal{H}
be a hypergraph. A
k
-clique embedding

\psi
to
\mathcal{H}
, denoted as
C_{k}\mapsto\mathcal{H}
, maps every vertex
v
in
C_{k}
to a non-empty subset
\psi(v)\subseteq V(\mathcal{H})
such that the followings hold:

(1)

\psi(v)
induces a connected subhypergraph;

(2)

for any
u,v
in
C_{k}
, either
\psi(u)\cap\psi(v)\neq\emptyset
or there exists a hyperedge
e\in E(\mathcal{H})
such that
e\cap\psi(u)\neq\emptyset
and
e\cap\psi(v)\neq\emptyset
.

In this paper, we care about the
weak edge depth
of a
k
-clique embedding
\psi
. Given a
k
-clique embedding
\psi
, we define
\forall e\in E(\mathcal{H})
the weak edge depth of
e
as
d_{\psi}(e):=|\{v\in V(C_{k})\mid\psi(v)\cap e\neq\emptyset\}|
. Then for a
k
-clique embedding, its weak edge depth is defined to be
\mathsf{wed}(\psi):=\max_{e}d_{\psi}(e)
. Finally, we define
\mathsf{wed}(\psi)
to be the minimum weak edge depth of any
k
-clique embedding to
\mathcal{H}
. We are now ready to define the
signed clique embedding power
.

Definition D.6 (Signed Clique Embedding Power).

Given a signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
, define the
signed clique embedding power
of
\mathcal{H}
as

\mathsf{semb}(\mathcal{H}):=\max_{H\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}\sup_{k\geq 3}\mathsf{semb}_{k}(H)=\max_{H\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}\sup_{k\geq 3}\frac{k}{\mathsf{wed}(C_{k}\mapsto H)}

where
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}
is the set of all possible hypergraphs with hyperedges
\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
where
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}
.

We first show that the singed clique embedding power for non-
signed
-acyclic queries is strictly greater than 1.

Theorem D.7.

If
\mathcal{H}
is a non-
signed
-acyclic signed hypergraph, then its signed clique embedding power is strictly greater than 1.

Proof of Theorem
D.7
.

Since
\mathcal{H}
is not
signed
-acyclic, there exists a subset
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}
such that the hypergraph
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}
is not
\alpha
-acyclic. By Theorem
D.3
, either
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is non-conformal or
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
contains an induced
\ell
-cycle for some
\ell\geq 4
.

If
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is non-conformal, take a
k
-clique,
k\geq 3
in its Gaifman graph such that no hyperedge in
q^{\prime}
covers the
k
-clique. Now we define the embedding from
C_{k}
to
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
to be any one-to-one mapping between the vertices in
C_{k}
and the
k
-clique in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
. The weak edge depth of this embedding must be strictly smaller than
k
, by the construction. Therefore,
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
has signed clique embedding power strictly greater than 1, and so does
\mathcal{H}
.

Otherwise, there exists an induced
\ell
-cycle for some
\ell\geq 4
. We take the same embedding from
(
Fan et al. 2023
)
. We name the variables of the cycle query as
x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}
. If
\ell
is odd, let
\lambda=(\ell+1)/2
and we construct the
\ell
-clique embedding as follows:

(21)

\displaystyle\psi^{-1}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=\{1,2,\dots,\lambda-1\}

\displaystyle\psi^{-1}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=\{2,3,\dots,\lambda\}

\displaystyle\dots

\displaystyle\psi^{-1}(x_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\{2\lambda-1,1,\dots,\lambda-2\}

If
\ell
is even, we define
\lambda=\ell/2
and an embedding from a
(\ell-1)
-clique as follows:

\displaystyle\psi^{-1}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=\{1,2,\dots,\lambda-1\}

\displaystyle\psi^{-1}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=\{2,3,\dots,\lambda\}

\displaystyle\dots

\displaystyle\psi^{-1}(x_{\ell-1})

\displaystyle=\{2\lambda-2,2\lambda-1,1,\dots,\lambda-3\}

\displaystyle\psi^{-1}(x_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\psi^{-1}(x_{\ell-1})

Observe that the weak edge depth of the embedding is strictly smaller than
\ell
. Therefore in this case
\mathcal{H}
also has signed clique embedding power strictly greater than 1.
∎

We now show that the signed clique embedding power provides a conditional lower bound for the running time of a
\mathsf{SumProd}^{\neg}
query over the tropical semiring, assuming Conjecture
6.6
.

Theorem D.8.

Assuming Conjecture
6.6
, any
\mathsf{SumProd}^{\neg}
query with signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
over the tropical semiring cannot be solved in time
O(N^{\mathsf{semb}(\mathcal{H})-\epsilon})
for any constant
\epsilon>0
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}
be a hypergraph such that for some
k\geq 3
there exists a
k
-clique embedding to
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
with weak edge depth
\lambda=\mathsf{semb}(\mathcal{H})
. From
(
Fan et al. 2023
)
, the
\mathsf{SumProd}
problem on the hypergraph
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
over the tropical semiring cannot be solved in time
O(N^{\lambda-\epsilon})
for any
\epsilon>0
assuming Conjecture
6.6
. Let
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
. We will show how to reduce
\mathsf{SumProd}
on
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{SumProd}^{\neg}
on
\mathcal{H}
(over the tropical semiring).

Given an input
I^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{SumProd}
on
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
, we construct an instance
I
(of the same size) for
\mathsf{SumProd}^{\neg}
on
\mathcal{H}
as follows. If
U\in\mathcal{E}^{+}
,
R_{U}
is the same positive factor as in
I^{\prime}
. If
U\in\mathcal{E}^{-}\setminus\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
,
R_{U}
is empty with default value
0
. Finally, if
U\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
, the negative factor
R_{U}
takes the same values as in
I^{\prime}
with default value a large enough constant
c>0
(we pick
c
such that no sum of
\ell
values from
I^{\prime}
can exceed it, where
\ell
is the number of atoms in the query).

It is easy to see that the minimum achieved for the
\mathsf{SumProd}^{\neg}
problem on
\mathcal{H}
remains the same.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.8
.

Let
Q
be the
\mathsf{SumProd}^{\neg}
query with signed hypergraph
([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
. Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that we can
compute
\#Q
in linear time.

We use the inclusion-exclusion principle. Let
Q_{S}
be the Boolean CQ with hypergraph
([n],\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup S)
for any
S\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}
.
Then, we can write:

\displaystyle\#Q(\mathcal{D})=\sum_{S\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}}(-1)^{|S|}\#Q_{S}(\mathcal{D})

Since
\mathcal{H}
is not
signed
-acyclic, there exists a minimal
U\subset\mathcal{E}^{-}
such that
Q_{U}
is not
\alpha
-acyclic.

We can now construct a linear-time reduction from
\#Q_{U}
to
\#Q
. Given an instance
\mathcal{D}
for
\#Q_{U}
, we construct an instance
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
for
\#Q
by copying
\mathcal{D}
for relations in
\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup U
, and making the relation empty if it is in
\mathcal{E}^{-}\setminus U
. In this case, we have:

\displaystyle\#Q(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})=\sum_{S\subseteq U}(-1)^{|S|}\#Q_{S}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})=\sum_{S\subset U}(-1)^{|S|}\#Q_{S}(\mathcal{D})+(-1)^{|U|}\#Q_{U}(\mathcal{D})

By minimality of
U
, all the queries
Q_{S}
with
S\subset U
are
\alpha
-acyclic and thus
\#Q_{S}(\mathcal{D})
can be computed in linear time.
Hence, a linear-time algorithm for
\#Q
implies a linear-time algorithm for
\#Q_{U}
, which is not
\alpha
-acyclic.
∎

D.4.
Missing proofs in Section
6.3

We prove Theorem
6.9
in this section. First, as noted in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
, it suffices to consider the commutative semigroup
(\mathcal{P}(X),\cup)
for any set
X
, where
\mathcal{P}
is the powerset of
X
. Indeed, any solution to a task
T
with
n
elements
\{a_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq n-1}
can be interpreted to the same task defined by
(\mathcal{P}([n]),\cup)

. Note that the semigroup
(\mathcal{P}([n]),\cup)
is faithful for any
n\geq 1
. It leads to the following definition in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
.

Definition D.9.

A scheme
S
is a sequence
s_{0},\dots,s_{r-1}
of subsets of
X
such that for all
i\in[0,r-1]
,
s_{i}=s\cup s^{\prime}
where
s=s_{j}
for some
j<i
or
s=\{x\}
for some
x\in X
or
s=\emptyset
, and likewise for
s^{\prime}
.

The following lemma is proved in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
for the partial sum case. The exact same proof works for OEPS.

Lemma D.10.

Let
T
be a task for OEPS over
n
variables and
S
a scheme of minimum length solving it. Then, for any faithful semigroup, a solution to
T
takes time at least
r-n
, where
r
is the length of the scheme.

Following
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
, we continue with the definition of mappings between schemes. These are often simply maps between sets
f:X\rightarrow Y
extended to maps between powersets
f:\mathcal{P}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(Y)
in the usual way: requiring that
f(A\cup B)=f(A)\cup f(B)
. Other times, we intend that the map be between intervals. We denote by
\mathcal{I}(X)
the set of all intervals in
\mathcal{P}(X)
,

\mathcal{I}(X):=\left\{\left[x_{i},x_{j}\right]\subseteq X\mid i\leq j\right\}

A map
f:X\rightarrow Y
extends to
f:\mathcal{I}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(Y)
by
f\left(\left[x_{i},x_{j}\right]\right)=\left[f\left(x_{i}\right),f\left(x_{j}\right)\right]
. It is often convenient to define a map by defining its inverse first. A section of a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a map
g:Y\rightarrow X
such that
f\circ g=I_{T}
.

We will construct a family
T_{n}(t,k)
of hard tasks parametrized by two integers
t\geq 0
and
k\geq 0
, called
time
and
density
respectively; the subscript
n
is the number of variables and is not a parameter. The construction mimics the one in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
, but extends it to be the semantics of OEPS in the obvious way. However, syntactically, readers can think that the tasks for (standard) partial-sum and OEPS are both sets of pairs of elements.

First, define the function
R(t,k)
for all integers
t\geq 1
and
k\geq 0

(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
:

\displaystyle R(1,k)=2k

\displaystyle\text{if }k\geq 0,

\displaystyle R(t,0)=3

\displaystyle\text{if }t>1

\displaystyle R(t,k)=R(t,k-1)\cdot R(t-1,R(t,k-1))

\displaystyle\text{if }k>0,t>1.

This function gives the
n
needed to construct the hard task
T_{n}(t,k)
.

The next lemma is crucial.

Lemma D.11.

For all integers
t\geq 1
and
k\geq 0
, there is a task
\mathcal{T}_{n}(t,k)
for OEPS over the
n
element set
X=\left\{x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1}\right\}
satisfying the three requirements:

(1)

\left|\mathcal{T}_{n}(t,k)\right|\geq kn/2
, where
n=R(t,k)
.

(2)

\left|\left\{(x_{i},x_{j})\in\mathcal{T}_{n}(t,k)\mid i=l\right\}\right|\leq k
for any
l\in[0,n-1]
.

(3)

If
S=\left\{s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{r-1}\right\}
is a scheme solving
\mathcal{T}_{n}(t,k)
, then
r\geq t\left|T_{n}(t,k)\right|/3
.

All intervals in
\mathcal{T}_{n}(t,k)
are nontrivial.

Our construction of tasks
T_{n}(t,k)
for OEPS is by double induction on
t
and
k
, which is similar to the construction in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
.
First, we show the construction for the base cases:

•

if
t\geq 2
and
k=0
, define
T_{3}(t,0):=\emptyset
;

•

if
t=1
and
k\geq 0
, we have
n=R(1,k)=2k
and the variable set is
\{x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}\}
. Define

T_{n}(1,k)=\bigcup\limits_{i=0}^{n-k-1}\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{k}\{(x_{i},x_{i+j})\}.

It can be easily verified that the base case constructions satisfy the three requirements posed in Lemma
D.11
. For example for the third requirement: when
k=0
, any scheme solving
T_{3}(t,0)
trivially has length longer than 0; when
t=1
and
k\geq 0
, any scheme solving
T_{n}(1,k)
must contain at least
|T_{n}(1,k)|
steps (in order to output
|T_{n}(1,k)|
many different sums), which is greater than
t\left|T_{n}(t,k)\right|/3=\left|T_{n}(1,k)\right|/3
since
t=1
.

We now construct the tasks when
k>0
and
t>1
. By induction hypothesis, we have tasks
A=\mathcal{T}_{a}(t,k-1)
and
B=\mathcal{T}_{b}(t-1,a)
where
a=R(t,k-1)
and
b=R(t-1,a)
.
Since
R(t,k)=ab
, we intend to construct task
Q=\mathcal{T}_{n}(t,k)
over
n=ab
variables.
For clarity, we give different names to all these different variable sets.
Name the variables in
Q
by
X=\left\{x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1}\right\}
, those in
A
by
Y=\left\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{a-1}\right\}
, those in
B
by
Z=\left\{z_{0},\ldots,z_{b-1}\right\}

Divide
X
into
b
blocks each containing
a
consecutive variables. In to each block, we place a copy of the task
A
. To state this formally, we define the map:

\varphi:\begin{array}[]{clc}\mathcal{P}(X)&\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(Y)\\
x_{i}&\mapsto y_{i\bmod a}\end{array}

and take these sections:

\displaystyle\varphi_{j}:\mathcal{I}(Y)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(X)

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{ja+i}

where
j=0,\ldots,b-1
. Each section gives a copy of
A
placed in
X
, it is the image of
A
by
\varphi_{j}
. Though
\varphi
is a map between subsets of sets, it is defined as if it were a map of sets. As in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
, we will not carry out in the notation the details for these distinctions, but the readers are encouraged to reflect on these distinctions.

We then mark some of the
x_{i}
’s. Let the leftmost variable in each block be marked, that is,
x_{iG}
for
i=0,1,\ldots,b-1
. Now alter the marking by removing the mark on
x_{0}
and placing it on
x_{n-1}
.

We now partition
B
into a subsets
B_{0},\ldots,B_{a-1}
so that the partition obeys the following restrictions:

(1)

\left|\left\{\left[z_{j},z_{k}\right]\in B_{i}\mid j=c\right\}\right|\leq 1
for all
i\in[0,a-1]
and
c\in[0,b-1]
.

(2)

\left[z_{b-2},z_{b-1}\right]\notin B_{0}
.

It is possible to construct this partition thanks to the uniform right-degree condition on
B
and that fact that there is only one nontrivial interval ending over
z_{b-2}
.
For
i\in[0,a-1]
define the map,

\displaystyle\psi_{i}:\mathcal{I}(Z)\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(X)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\left[z_{j}\right]\mapsto\begin{cases}{\left[x_{(j+1)a-i},x_{(j+1)a}\right]}&\text{ for }j\in[0,b-2]\\
{\left[x_{ab-1}\right]}&\text{ if }j=b-1.\end{cases}}

Continuing as in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
, we now define a map of intervals. To this end we shall ensure that
\psi_{i}\left(\left[z_{j},z_{k}\right]\right)
is the smallest interval containing all of
\left\{\psi_{i}\left(z_{j}\right),\psi_{i}\left(z_{k}\right)\right\}
. Each of these is a section of the map,

\displaystyle\psi:\mathcal{P}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(Z)

\displaystyle x_{i}\mapsto\begin{cases}z_{j}&\text{ if }i=a(j+1),\\
z_{b-1}&\text{ if }i=ab-1,\\
\emptyset&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

An image of each
B_{i}
is placed over
X
using
\psi_{i}
. Remark that any such interval over
X
spans blocks. For this to be true, the restriction that
\left[z_{b-2},z_{b-1}\right]
not be in
B_{0}
is crucial.

Finally, the task
Q
is defined as,

Q:=\left(\bigcup_{j=0}^{b-1}\varphi_{j}(A)\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{i=0}^{a-1}\psi_{i}\left(B_{i}\right)\right).

It is not hard to show that the task
Q
satisfies the first two requirements in Lemma
D.11
. Indeed, by construction,
Q
is a collection of nontrivial intervals in
X
. In Iact, eacn map
\varphi_{j}
and
\psi_{i}
is one-to-one. The distinct character of each of these maps assures that each component that went into making
Q
is disjoint with every other. This implies that

\displaystyle|Q|

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[0,b-1]}\left|\varphi_{j}(A)\right|+\sum_{i\in[0,a-1]}\left|\psi_{i}\left(B_{i}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=b|A|+|B|\geq b(k-1)a/2+ab/2=kab/2

and

\displaystyle\left|\left\{\left(x_{i},x_{j}\right)\in Q\mid i=c\right\}\right|=

\displaystyle\left|\left\{\left(y_{i},y_{j}\right)\in A\mid i=c\bmod a\right\}\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\left\{\left(z_{i},z_{j}\right)\in B_{(-c)\bmod a}\mid i=\lfloor(c-1)/a\rfloor\right\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(k-1)+1=k

for all
c\in[0,ab-1]
. Therefore,
Q
is a task of the correct density and obeys the uniform right-degree condition.

Our observation is that the lower bound in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
still works whitebox-wise. Intuitively, for fixed set of pairs, the OEPS computes strictly more than the standard partial sums, and therefore it should not be easier to compute the OEPS. Mathematically, we need to carefully examine that, indeed, no “magical shortcut” is possible. We have the following lemmas, whose proof follows the proofs of Lemma 3 and Lemma 4 in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
.

As in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
, we stratify
s_{j}
’s according to whether they consist of “cross-block” compositions. Specifically, each of the
s_{j}
in scheme
S
falls into one of
b+1
categories. Either, for some
i\in[0,b-1]
,
s_{j}
lies fully inside block
i,s_{j}\subseteq\left[x_{ia},x_{(i+1)a-1}\right]
, or
s_{j}
combines elements from different blocks. We partition
S
into
b+1
sublists according to this categorization. If
s_{j}
lies inside block
i
, place
s_{j}
in
S^{i}
, else place
s_{j}
in
S^{b}
. If
s_{j}
is the empty set, place it in the subsequence
S^{b}
. Maintain the original ordering, but renumber, to obtain sublists
\left\{s_{0}^{i},s_{1}^{i},\ldots,s_{r_{i}-1}^{i}\right\}
where
i=0,\ldots,b
, each
s_{j}^{i}
is an element of
\mathcal{P}(X)
, and the
r_{i}
are the lengths of these lists, noting
r=r_{0}+\cdots+r_{b}
.

Lemma D.12.

For
i=0,\ldots,b-1
, the sequences
\varphi\left(S^{i}\right)
, defined by
\varphi\left(S^{i}\right)_{j}=\varphi\left(s_{j}^{i}\right)
, are schemes solving
A
.

Before presenting the proof, we note an important difference between the proof of Lemma
D.12
in the
OEPS
setting and the proof of Lemma 3 in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
(although the difference is only mentally significant while the proof is almost verbatim). In the proof of Lemma 3 in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
, there is an exact correspondence between the sequences
\varphi\left(S^{i}\right)
where
i=0,\ldots,b-1
and the solution of
A
, in the sense that
\varphi\left(S^{i}\right)
is a solution for
A
while its preimage
S^{i}
is also a solution for
\varphi_{i}(A)
in
Q
. However, in
OEPS
setting, although
\varphi\left(S^{i}\right)
is a solution for
A
, its preimage
S^{i}
is not a solution for
\varphi_{i}(A)
in
Q
, since by the definition of
OEPS
a solution for the latter needs to compute sums outside the block
i
. It is in this precise sense that giving the unconditional lower bound for the off-line mode partial sum, no “magical shortcut” is possible.

Proof of Lemma
D.12
.

Let
s_{\alpha}
be any member of
\varphi\left(S^{i}\right)
. Then
s_{\alpha}
is the image of some element
s_{j}
in
S
. We demonstrate the case
s_{j}=s_{k}\cup s_{l}
with
k,l<j
. Clearly,
s_{k}
and
s_{l}
fall into
S^{i}
with images
s_{\beta}
and
s_{\gamma}
in
\varphi\left(S^{i}\right)
, where
\beta,\gamma<\alpha
. But

s_{\alpha}=\varphi\left(s_{j}\right)=\varphi\left(s_{k}\cup s_{l}\right)=\varphi\left(s_{k}\right)\cup\varphi\left(s_{l}\right)=s_{\beta}\cup s_{\gamma}

The other possible precursors of
s_{j}
are argued similarly, proving that
\varphi\left(S^{i}\right)
is a scheme. We know that
\varphi_{i}
is a section of
\varphi
and that
S
solves
Q
. This implies
S^{i}\supseteq\varphi_{i}\left(\mathcal{T}_{a}(t,k-1)\right)
, giving

\varphi\left(S^{i}\right)\supseteq\varphi\circ\varphi_{i}\left(T_{a}(t,k-1)\right)=T_{a}(t,k-1)

So
\varphi\left(S^{i}\right)
solves
T_{a}(t,k-1)
, i.e.
A
.
∎

Lemma D.13.

The sequence
\psi\left(S^{b}\right)
, defined by

(1)

\psi\left(S^{b}\right)_{i+1}=\psi\left(s_{i}^{b}\right)
,

(2)

\psi\left(S^{b}\right)_{0}=\left[z_{b-2},z_{b-1}\right]

is a scheme solving
B
. It is not minimal: there is a subsequence of
\psi\left(S^{b}\right)
, resulting from the removal of
|B|-\left|B_{0}\right|
elements from
\psi\left(S^{b}\right)
, which is also a scheme solving B.

Proof.

The fact that
\psi\left(S^{b}\right)
is a scheme that solves
B
is similar as the proof of Lemma
D.12
.

Let
q
be an interval in the task
Q
of the form
\psi_{i}(z)
, but
i\neq 0
. That is,
q
is in the image of
B
, but not of that portion of
B
placed in the partition
B_{0}
. Let
i(q)
be the index of the leftmost
q_{i}
in
q
. Let
W(i)
be the index of the first element in
S^{b}
which contains
q_{i}
but contains no
q_{j}
with
j<i
. Since
S
solves
Q,W(i)
is defined. We consider the equation
s_{W(i)}^{b}=s^{\prime}\cup s^{\prime\prime}
. At least one of
s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime\prime}
contains
q_{i}
; without loss of generality assume it is
s^{\prime}
. By selection of
W(i),s^{\prime}
is itself contained in the block containing
q_{i}
. Because
i
is not divisible by
a
, the image of this set under
\psi
must be the empty set. (It cannot be that this set contains
x_{n-1}
.) Hence
\psi\left(s_{W(i)}^{b}\right)
either appeared before in
\psi\left(S^{b}\right)
or, because
\psi\left(S^{b}\right)
is a scheme, it is a singleton. In either case, we can remove this element from the sequence
\psi\left(S^{b}\right)
and it still will be a scheme. After removal, it still will solve
B
since that set contains no singletons.
∎

Following
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
, a lower bound on the length of
S
can be derived. Because
\varphi\left(S^{i}\right)
solves
A
,
r_{i}\geq t|A|/3,i=0,\ldots,b-1
. Taking the indicated subsequence of
\psi\left(S^{b}\right)
, we have a scheme of size
r_{b}+1-|B|+\left|B_{0}\right|
solving
B
. Therefore, that sum is bound below by
(t-1)|B|/3
. Recall that each interval in
B_{0}
ends over one of
z_{0},\ldots,z_{b-3}
, and conversely, each
z_{0},\ldots,z_{b-3}
has at most one interval in
B_{0}
ending over it. Hence
\left|B_{0}\right|\leq b-2
. Since
|Q|=b|A|+|B|

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=r_{0}+\cdots+r_{b-1}+r_{b}

\displaystyle\geq bt|A|/3+(t-1)|B|/3+|B|-\left|B_{0}\right|-1

\displaystyle\geq bt|A|/3+(t+2)|B|/3-(b-2)-1

\displaystyle=t|Q|/3+(2/3)|B|-b+1

Because
B=\mathcal{T}_{b}(t-1,a)
we have
|B|\geq ab/2
. Since
a\geq 3

r\geq t|Q|/3+ab/3-b+1>t|Q|/3

We have completed the verification of the three properties of
T_{n}(t,k)
listed in Lemma
D.11
, and the induction step is complete.

Define the function
A(i,j)
for all
i\geq 1
and
j\geq 0
as in
(
Tarjan 1975
)
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}A(1,j)=2^{j},&j\geq 0\\
A(i,0)=2,&i>1\\
A(i,j)=A(i-1,A(i,j-1)),&j>0,i>1.\end{array}

The following two lemmas are proved in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
relating
R(i,j)
and
A(i,j)
.

Lemma D.14.

For all
i,j=1,2,\ldots
, we have
R(i+1,j)>A(i,j)
.

Lemma D.15.

For all
i,j=1,2,\ldots
, we have
A(i+2,j)>R(i,j)
.

Finally, we proceed as in
(
Chazelle and Rosenberg 1991
)
to finish the preparation for proving Theorem
6.9
. Suppose we are given
m
and
n
with
m\geq n
. Set
k=\lfloor m/n\rfloor
and let
t
be the least integer such that
R(t,k)>n
. We explain why
t\geq 2
. A task cannot repeat a query interval, so
m\leq n(n-1)/2
, and hence,

R(1,k)=2k\leq 2\lfloor m/n\rfloor\leq n-1

By the lower bound lemma, there exists a task
T=T_{n^{\prime}}(t-1,k)
with

•

n^{\prime}=R(t-1,k)
, and it follows by the definition of
t
that
n^{\prime}<n
.

•

T
has size
|T|
between
kn^{\prime}/2
and
kn^{\prime}
.

•

Any solution to
T
has length at least
(t-1)|T|/3
.

Place
\left[n/n^{\prime}\right]
copies of
T
side by side. Add extra variables and queries to correct to form of this resultant task, that is, it will be over
n
variables and have
m
queries. The size of any solution is bounded from below by:

\displaystyle(t-1)\left\lfloor n/n^{\prime}\right\rfloor|T|/3

\displaystyle\geq(t-1)\left\lfloor n/n^{\prime}\right\rfloor\lfloor m/n\rfloor n^{\prime}/6

\displaystyle\geq m(t-1)/24

\displaystyle\geq mt/48.

The following chain of inequalities shows that
t+2\geq\alpha(m,n)
,

A(t+2,k)>R(t,k)>n>\log n

Because
t\geq 2
, it follows that
t\geq\alpha(m,n)/2
. Therefore the cost of solving
T
is at least
m\alpha(m,n)/96
.

Consider now
m
and
n
given, where
m<n
. The previous paragraphs shows the existence of a task
T
over
m
variables with
m
queries which takes time at least
(m\alpha(m,m))/96
to solve. If all the variables
x_{0},\ldots,x_{m-1}
do not appear in
T
, eliminate the unused variables and renumber as
x_{0},\ldots,x_{m-1}
. Add variables
x_{m^{\prime}},\ldots,x_{n-1}
. Find an interval of the form
\left[i,m^{\prime}-1\right]
in
T
, as
x_{m^{\prime}-1}
is used in
T
such an interval exists, and replace it with the interval
[i,n-1]
. Any solution to the resulting task can be made to solve the original task
T
by a transformation which includes removing the at least
n-m
computation steps referencing variables with indices inside the interval
[m,n-1]
. So, the time to solve this new task must be,

\displaystyle n-m+m\alpha(m,m)/96

\displaystyle\geq(n-m)/192+m\alpha(m,m)/96

\displaystyle\geq n/192+m(2\alpha(m,m)-1)/192

\displaystyle\geq(n+m\alpha(m,m))/192.

We are now ready to prove Theorem
6.9
.

Proof of Theorem
6.9
.

We show that any constant delay enumeration algorithm for
\varphi
with preprocessing time
T
leads to an algorithm for
OEPS
that runs in time
T+O(m+n)
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}
and
(l_{1},r_{1})
,
(l_{2},r_{2})
,
\dots
,
(l_{m},r_{m})
be inputs to the
\mathsf{OpenEndedPartialSum}
problem where
m\leq n
.

We first construct factors
A
,
B
, and
R
as follows: Fix a domain
D=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
, and

•

for every
i\in D
,
A(i)=\mathbf{1}
, the multiplicative identity under the semiring;

•

for every
j\in D
,
B(j)=a_{j}
;

•

for every
k\in\{1,2,\dots,m\}
,
R(k,l_{k})=\mathbf{1}
and
R(k,r_{k})=\mathbf{1}
.

This construction runs in time
O(n+2m)
.
Then, we simply compute
\varphi
in time
T+O(n)
.
Note that for any
i\in\{1,2,\dots,m\}
, the open ended sum for
(l_{i},r_{i})
is stored in
\phi(i)
:

\varphi(i)=\bigoplus_{1\leq j\leq n}A(i)\otimes B(j)\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{{\lnot R}}(i,j)=\bigoplus_{j\in[n]\setminus\{l_{i},r_{i}\}}a_{j}.

Hence the overall running time of this algorithm is
T+O(n+2m)
.
By Theorem
6.9
, we have that
T+O(n+2m)=\Omega(n+m\cdot\alpha(m,m))
.
Therefore,
T=\Omega(m\cdot\alpha(m,m))
, as desired.
∎

Appendix E
Missing proofs in Section
7

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

Following
(
Hu and Wang 2023
)
, we can write
Q
as follows:

Q=\bigcup_{R_{e}\in Q_{2}}(\bigwedge_{S_{e}\in Q_{1}}S_{e}\wedge\neg R_{e})

Hence,
Q
can be viewed as a union of
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
, each
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
of the form
Q_{1}\wedge\neg R_{e}
. Since
Q_{1}
is
\alpha
-acyclic and
Q_{1}\wedge R_{e}
is
\alpha
-acyclic,
Q_{1}\wedge\neg R_{e}
is
signed
-acyclic and free-connex (trivially, since it is full) and thus by Theorem
1.1
can be enumerated with linear-time preprocessing and constant delay. We can now apply the Cheater’s Lemma
(
Carmeli and Kröll 2021
)
to obtain linear-time preprocessing and constant-delay enumeration for
Q
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
7.2
.

From
(
Hu and Wang 2023
)
, we can reduce
Q
to a difference
Q_{1}^{\prime}-Q_{2}^{\prime}
in linear time such that
Q_{1}^{\prime},Q_{2}^{\prime}
are full CQs and satisfy the properties of Theorem
7.1
. We then simply apply Theorem
7.1
.
∎

Appendix F
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
with Bounded Width

What can we say about the data complexity of evaluating a
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
if it is not
signed
-acyclic? Lanzinger
(
Lanzinger 2021
)
defined a width measure of a
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
called
nest-set width (
\mathsf{nsw}
)
and showed that a Boolean
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
with bounded
\mathsf{nsw}
can be computed in polynomial time (combined complexity). One consequence of (
2
) is that it is possible to obtain in a straightforward way a much tighter upper bound in terms of data complexity.

Definition F.1.

Let
\varphi
be a
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
with signed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}=([n],\mathcal{E}^{+},\mathcal{E}^{-})
. Then,
\beta
-
\#\mathsf{subw}(\varphi)=\max_{S\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{-}}\#\mathsf{subw}(\varphi_{S})
, where
\varphi_{S}
is the CQ with hypergraph
([n],\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup S)
and
\#\mathsf{subw}(\varphi)
is the
counting
version of the submodular width defined in
(
Khamis et al. 2020
)
.

Thanks to
(
Abo Khamis et al. 2017
;
Khamis et al. 2020
)
, for any Boolean CQ
\varphi
, we can compute
\#\varphi
in time
\tilde{O}(|\mathcal{D}|^{\#\mathsf{subw}(\varphi)})
, where
\tilde{O}
hides a polylogarithmic factor in
|\mathcal{D}|
. Combining this with (
2
), we have:

Theorem F.2.

Let
\varphi
be a Boolean
\mathsf{CQ}^{\neg}
. Then, we can compute
\#\varphi
(and thus
\varphi
) in time
\tilde{O}(|\mathcal{D}|^{\beta\text{-}\#\mathsf{subw}(\varphi)})

From
(
Lanzinger 2021
)
, we know that
\beta
-
\#\mathsf{subw}
is always at most the nest-set width; moreover, there are queries were nest-set width is unbounded but
\beta
-
\#\mathsf{subw}
is bounded.
